<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct a finite translational tile in ℤ<sup>3</sup> that admits tilings but no fully periodic tiling. Its unit-cube thickening has the same property in ℝ<sup>3</sup>, even when arbitrary real translations are allowed. This gives a negative resolution of the periodic tiling conjecture in dimension three.

- **C-thm-main** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a finite nonempty set $T\subset\mathbb Z^3$ such that:* 1. *$T$ tiles $\mathbb Z^3$ by translations, but no translation set $A$ with $A\oplus T=\mathbb Z^3$ is invariant under a subgroup of finite index in $\mathbb Z^3$;* 2. *the set $\Omega=T+[0,1]^3$ tiles $\mathbb R^3$ by translations up to null sets, but no such tiling has a translation set invariant under a lattice of full rank in $\mathbb R^3$.
  - Explanation: A finite translational tile admits tilings while excluding every fully periodic translation set, both on the integer lattice and for its unit-cube thickening.
- **C-word-affine** (theorem, [source](#stmt-word-affine)): If $W$ satisfies the line rule, there are $A,B,C\in\mathbb F_p$, not all zero, such that $$W(n,m)=An+Bm+C \quad\text{whenever }An+Bm+C\ne0.$$
  - Explanation: The line rule forces agreement with a global affine form wherever that form is nonzero.
- **C-word-vertical** (theorem, [source](#stmt-word-vertical)): Let $H(n,m)=An+Bm+C$ be any affine form supplied by <a href="#word:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:affine">3</a>. Every column of $W$ is nonconstant if and only if $B\ne0$. In that case every positive vertical period of $W$ is divisible by $p$.
  - Explanation: The vertical coefficient characterizes nonconstant columns and forces divisibility of any vertical period.
- **C-word-obstruction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-word-obstruction)): There is no array $W\colon I\times\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$ satisfying the line rule, having every column nonconstant, and having a positive vertical period.
  - Explanation: Minimal-period descent excludes vertically periodic line-rule arrays with nonconstant columns.
- **C-word-model** (construction, [source](#stmt-word-model)): For $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, let $\operatorname{ord}_p(t)$ be the largest nonnegative integer $r$ for which $p^r\mid t$, and define $$f(t)=\frac{t}{p^{\operatorname{ord}_p(t)}}\pmod p, \qquad f(0)=1.$$ Then $f\colon\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$, and the array $W(n,m)=f(m)$ satisfies the line rule and has every column nonconstant.
  - Explanation: The last-nonzero-digit function gives a line-rule array with nonconstant columns, including at negative arguments and at zero.
- **C-enc-invariance** (construction, [source](#stmt-enc-invariance)): Fix a channel $i$ and a shift $\sigma\in\mathbb Z^2\times K$. There is a finite nonempty tile $F_{i,\sigma}\subset G$ such that, for every graph satisfying <a href="#enc:graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:graph">[enc:graph]</a>, $$A\oplus F_{i,\sigma}=G \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad c_i(b-\sigma)=c_i(b)\text{ for every }b\in\mathbb Z^2\times K.$$ This equation imposes no restriction on the graph’s other outputs.
  - Explanation: A finite tile imposes exactly the specified useful-output invariance while leaving other outputs unrestricted.
- **C-enc-exclusion** (construction, [source](#stmt-enc-exclusion)): Let $C=(i_1,\ldots,i_t)$ be a nonempty cyclically ordered list of distinct channels, and choose a label $j(i)\in J_i$ for each $i\in C$. There is a finite family of finite nonempty tiles such that a graph satisfying <a href="#enc:dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:dependence">[enc:dependence]</a> tiles with every member of this family if and only if, at every $x\in\mathbb Z^2$, at least one of the chosen labels $j(i)$ is inactive.
  - Explanation: Cycle tests encode the exclusion of simultaneous activity of the specified labels.
- **C-act-fibre-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-act-fibre-criterion)): Suppose $A\oplus H_0=G$, so that $A$ is a graph over $(x,k)$. For a target base point $(x,k)$ and a batch with horizontal offset $h$, write $$\label{act:source-base} x'=x-h,\qquad k_i'=k_i-l,\qquad k_\ell'=k_\ell\quad(\ell\ne i).$$ For an ordinary channel $i$, the equation $A\oplus F_i^{\mathrm{act}}=G$ holds if and only if, at every target base point, the map $$\label{act:ordinary-map} (l,\delta)\longmapsto \bigl(c_i(x',k')+e_\delta,\,\beta_i(x',k')\bigr)$$ is a bijection from $K_i\times\Delta_i$ to $P_i\times K_i$. For a seed $i$, the corresponding necessary and sufficient condition is that, at every target base point, $$\label{act:seed-map} (l,\delta,\tau)\longmapsto \bigl(c_i(x',k')+e_\delta,\,\beta_i(x',k'),\,z(x',k')\bigr)$$ be a bijection from $K_i\times\Delta_i\times S$ to $P_i\times K_i\times\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z$.
  - Explanation: Exact tiling coverage is equivalent to a bijection of batch indices onto the constrained fibre coordinates.
- **C-act-ordinary** (theorem, [source](#stmt-act-ordinary)): Every ordinary channel has at least one active label at every $x\in\mathbb Z^2$ in any common solution of the equations specified so far.
  - Explanation: Nonconstant shift multiplicities force an active label at every ordinary-channel base point.
- **C-act-seed** (theorem, [source](#stmt-act-seed)): Each of the two seeds has its label active at some point of $\mathbb Z^2$ in any common solution of the equations specified so far.
  - Explanation: The seed histogram cannot satisfy the required coverage counts while remaining inactive everywhere.
- **C-act-nonperiodic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-act-nonperiodic)): No common solution of this finite system is invariant under a finite-index subgroup of $G$.
  - Explanation: A fully periodic common solution would decode into a forbidden vertically periodic line-rule array.
- **C-model-block-inverse** (construction, [source](#stmt-model-block-inverse)): Fix $i,j$, a target horizontal coordinate $x_1\in\mathbb Z$, and a target low coordinate $k_i\in K_i$. For each $(l,\delta)\in K_i\times\Delta_i$, choose an integer $h_1(l,\delta)$ satisfying $$h_1(l,\delta)\equiv l\pmod{r_i},\qquad h_1(l,\delta)\equiv\rho^a(\delta)\pmod{a_i},\qquad h_1(l,\delta)\equiv\rho^b(\delta)\pmod{b_i}.$$ Then the map $$\label{model:block-map} (l,\delta)\longmapsto \left( C_{i,j}(k_i-l)+e_\delta, T_{i,j}\bigl(x_1-h_1(l,\delta)\bigr)(k_i-l) \right)$$ is a bijection from $K_i\times\Delta_i$ to $P_i\times K_i$.
  - Explanation: The labelled-block rotations and fibre orderings admit a unique inverse for each output pair.
- **C-model-system** (construction, [source](#stmt-model-system)): There are a finite cyclic group $V$ and finitely many nonempty finite sets $F_1,\ldots,F_s\subset G=\mathbb Z^2\times V$ for which the simultaneous tiling equations $$A\oplus F_\nu=G\qquad(1\leq\nu\leq s)$$ have a common solution, but no common solution is invariant under a finite-index subgroup of $G$.
  - Explanation: The graph, dependence, exclusion and activation equations share a common solution in a group with cyclic finite factor, but no fully periodic common solution.
- **C-stack-partition** (construction, [source](#stmt-stack-partition)): For every positive integer $s$ and every finite set of primes, there is a prime $q$ outside that set and a partition $$\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z=E_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup E_s$$ into nonempty sets satisfying $E_\nu-E_\nu=\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ for every $\nu$.
  - Explanation: A sufficiently large fresh prime supports a partition whose colour classes each realize every difference.
- **C-stack-equivalence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-stack-equivalence)): Let $H$ be an abelian group, let $F_1,\ldots,F_s\subset H$ be finite nonempty sets, and let $E_1,\ldots,E_s$ be a partition as in <a href="#stack:partition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:partition">15</a>. Define $$F^{\mathrm{st}}=\bigcup_{\nu=1}^s(F_\nu\times E_\nu) \subset H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z.$$ There is a common solution to $A\oplus F_\nu=H$ for all $\nu$ if and only if $F^{\mathrm{st}}$ tiles $H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$. Moreover, a fully periodic tiling by $F^{\mathrm{st}}$ gives a fully periodic common solution, and a fully periodic common solution gives a fully periodic tiling by $F^{\mathrm{st}}$.
  - Explanation: Stacking turns simultaneous equations into one tile and preserves existence of fully periodic solutions in both directions.
- **C-stack-cyclic** (construction, [source](#stmt-stack-cyclic)): For some positive integer $Q$, there is a finite nonempty set $F\subset\Gamma=\mathbb Z^2\times\mathbb Z/Q\mathbb Z$ which tiles $\Gamma$ but admits no fully periodic tiling.
  - Explanation: A fresh coprime prime consolidates the system into a single tile while keeping the finite factor cyclic.
- **C-geom-random-representatives** (construction, [source](#stmt-geom-random-representatives)): For a sufficiently large integer $m\geq2$, one can choose $$T_0=\{t(v):v\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}^3\},\qquad t(v)\equiv v\pmod m,$$ such that, with $T_*=T_0\setminus\{t(0)\}$, $$T_*-T_*\supset \{d\in\mathbb Z^3:\norm{d}_\infty\leq m, \ d\notin m\mathbb Z^3\}. \label{geom:difference-property}$$
  - Explanation: A residue transversal can be selected whose common part realizes the required bounded nongrid differences.
- **C-geom-existence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-existence)): The tile $T$ tiles $\mathbb Z^3$, and $\Omega$ tiles $\mathbb R^3$ up to null sets.
  - Explanation: Kernel invariance makes the marked-point switch preserve coverage and yields both the discrete and thickened tilings.
- **C-geom-rounding** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-rounding)): Let $m\geq2$, and suppose that $\delta\in\mathbb R^3$ satisfies $\norm{\delta}_\infty\leq m$ and $\delta\notin m\mathbb Z^3$. There is $d\in\mathbb Z^3\setminus m\mathbb Z^3$ such that $$\norm{d}_\infty\leq m, \qquad \norm{\delta-d}_\infty<1.$$
  - Explanation: A nongrid vector in the closed box has a nearby integer nongrid vector satisfying the strict distance bound.
- **C-geom-grid** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-grid)): Suppose copies $c+\Omega_{\iota(c)}$, with types $\iota(c)\in\{0,1\}$, form an almost-everywhere tiling of $\mathbb R^3$. Assume the assembly, including its types, is invariant under a full-rank lattice $\Lambda\subset\mathbb R^3$. Then the set $C$ of centers is $c_0+m\mathbb Z^3$ for some $c_0\in\mathbb R^3$.
  - Explanation: Full-rank periodicity of the typed assembly forces its constituent centers to fill one translated grid.
- **C-geom-no-periodic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-no-periodic)): The Euclidean tile $\Omega$ has no almost-everywhere translational tiling whose translation set is invariant under a full-rank lattice in $\mathbb R^3$.
  - Explanation: Grid rigidity and marked-cell coverage force kernel invariance, enabling descent to the forbidden fully periodic quotient tiling.

        "anchor": "stmt-stack-equivalence",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-stack-equivalence",
          "exact_quote": "Let $H$ be an abelian group, let $F_1,\\ldots,F_s\\subset H$ be finite nonempty sets, and let $E_1,\\ldots,E_s$ be a partition as in <a href=\"#stack:partition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"stack:partition\">15</a>. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Exact translational tiling** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For subsets $A,F$ of an abelian group $G$, write $A\oplus F=G$ when every element of $G$ has a unique expression $a+f$ with $a\in A$ and $f\in F$.
- **Translational tile** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A finite nonempty set $F$ is a *translational tile* if such a set $A$ exists.
- **Fully periodic tiling** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A tiling is *fully periodic* if $A$ is invariant under a subgroup of finite index in $G$.
- **Euclidean full periodicity** ([source](#sec:introduction)): In $\mathbb R^3$, a bounded measurable set of positive measure tiles by translations when its translates partition space up to null sets; full periodicity means that its translation set is invariant under a lattice of full rank.
- **Allowed word** ([source](#stmt-word-definition)): A word $w\colon I\to\Sigma$ is *allowed* if there are integers $a,b$, not both divisible by $p$, such that, for every $n\in I$, $$w(n)= \begin{cases} an+b\pmod p, & p\nmid an+b,\\[2pt] (an+b)/p\pmod p, & p\mid an+b\text{ and }p^2\nmid an+b. \end{cases}$$ At indices with $p^2\mid an+b$, the value may be any member of $\Sigma$.
- **Line rule** ([source](#stmt-word-definition)): An array $W\colon I\times\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$ satisfies the *line rule* if $$n\longmapsto W(n,dn+e)$$ is an allowed word for every $d,e\in\mathbb Z$.
- **Positive vertical period** ([source](#stmt-word-definition)): A *positive vertical period* of $W$ is an integer $M>0$ such that $W(n,m+M)=W(n,m)$ for all $(n,m)\in I\times\mathbb Z$.
- **Last-nonzero-digit function** ([source](#stmt-word-model)): For $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, let $\operatorname{ord}_p(t)$ be the largest nonnegative integer $r$ for which $p^r\mid t$, and define $$f(t)=\frac{t}{p^{\operatorname{ord}_p(t)}}\pmod p, \qquad f(0)=1.$$
- **Final discrete tile and thickening** ([source](#geom:large-tile)): Our final discrete tile is $$T=T_1\cup\bigcup_{u\in U\setminus\{0\}}(mu+T_0), \qquad \Omega=T+[0,1]^3. \label{geom:large-tile}$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **affine** [claim](#stmt-word-affine) / [proof](#proof-stmt-word-affine): Find a nonexceptional square and propagate affine agreement. -> Global affine approximation
- **vertical** [claim](#stmt-word-vertical) / [proof](#proof-stmt-word-vertical): Compare nonzero affine values along each column. -> Vertical nondegeneracy and period divisibility
- **descent** [claim](#stmt-word-obstruction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-word-obstruction): Normalize the affine form and rescale a putative minimal period. -> No positive vertical period with nonconstant columns
- **model** [claim](#stmt-word-model) / [proof](#proof-stmt-word-model): Remove prime-power factors from integer arguments. -> Explicit line-rule array with nonconstant columns
- **dependence** [claim](#stmt-enc-invariance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-enc-invariance): Partition a graph fibre by a useful-output coordinate. -> Finite tiles imposing output invariance
- **exclusion** [claim](#stmt-enc-exclusion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-enc-exclusion): Test cyclic dependencies through finite permutation maps. -> Finite tests excluding forbidden simultaneous activity
- **activation** [claim](#stmt-act-nonperiodic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-act-nonperiodic): Force ordinary and seed activity, then decode a periodic common solution. -> No fully periodic common solution
- **blocks** [claim](#stmt-model-block-inverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-model-block-inverse): Recover the shift index and block label from each target. -> Exact inverse for ordinary activation
- **system** [claim](#stmt-model-system) / [proof](#proof-stmt-model-system): Construct ordinary outputs and seed outputs using the same auxiliary coordinate. -> Common solution with cyclic finite factor
- **partition** [claim](#stmt-stack-partition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-stack-partition): Use disjoint pairs and a union bound, then select a successful colouring. -> Nonempty partition with full difference sets
- **stack** [claim](#stmt-stack-equivalence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-stack-equivalence): Count fibre contributions and project the stacked translation set. -> Stacking equivalence preserving full periodicity
- **cyclic** [claim](#stmt-stack-cyclic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-stack-cyclic): Choose the stacking prime coprime to the finite coordinate order. -> Single quotient tile with no fully periodic tiling
- **rigid** [claim](#stmt-geom-random-representatives) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-random-representatives): Select disjoint residue pairs and bound failure by independent trials. -> Common-part transversal with the required difference property
- **existence** [claim](#stmt-geom-existence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-existence): Lift the quotient tiling and relocate marked points along the kernel. -> Discrete and Euclidean tilings
- **rounding** [claim](#stmt-geom-rounding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-rounding): Round noninteger coordinates while retaining a nongrid coordinate. -> Integer nongrid displacement with strict approximation
- **grid** [claim](#stmt-geom-grid) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-grid): Combine local overlap exclusion with periodic volume accounting and connected auxiliary cubes. -> Centers fill a translated grid
- **descent-geometry** [claim](#stmt-geom-no-periodic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-no-periodic): Read marked-cell coverage, obtain kernel invariance and descend a hypothetical periodic tiling. -> No fully periodic Euclidean tiling

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-round**: A vector in the closed box outside the coarse grid has an integer approximation outside that grid at strictly smaller than unit sup-norm distance.
- **I-grid**: The grid-center conclusion assumes a periodic assembly with preserved constituent types.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-partial**: Exclude every individual translation period of the tile. -> The conclusion excludes full periodicity and does not exclude each individual nonzero period.
- **A-grid**: Infer grid centers for an arbitrary nonperiodic constituent assembly. -> The coverage argument for auxiliary cubes uses periodicity of the typed assembly.
- **A-finite**: Treat a finite rounding check as a certificate for the entire tiling counterexample. -> Rounding alone does not construct the common tiling system or establish the periodicity obstruction.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The rounding proof gives a finite selection procedure for an integer approximation; this implementation checks that local construction and does not implement the entire tile.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON with integer m and denominator.
# PRECONDITIONS: m >= 2; positive denominator; bounded admitted grids.
# OUTPUTS: An integer rounding witness for every nongrid rational vector.
# TIME: Cubic in the number of coordinate samples; exact integer operations.
# SPACE: Cubic in the number of coordinate samples for the witness list.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact rational and integer arithmetic.
# FAILURE: Reject invalid input; no assertion about irrational inputs.
import json, sys
from itertools import product
from flint import fmpq
config = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
m, denominator = config['m'], config['denominator']
assert type(m) is int and type(denominator) is int
assert 2 <= m <= 3 and 1 <= denominator <= 3
samples = range(-m * denominator, m * denominator + 1)
rows = []
for numerators in product(samples, repeat=3):
    values = [fmpq(n, denominator) for n in numerators]
    if all(v.q == 1 and int(v.p) % m == 0 for v in values):
        continue
    rounded = [int(v.p) // int(v.q) for v in values]
    if all(z % m == 0 for z in rounded):
        for coordinate, v in enumerate(values):
            if v.q != 1:
                rounded[coordinate] += 1
                break
    rows.append([list(numerators), rounded])
print(json.dumps(rows, separators=(',', ':')))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |

| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction concerns exact translational tilings of an infinite lattice and Euclidean space. A correspondence with software correctness or execution behaviour is ABSENT. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The tile supplies a mathematical obstruction to full periodicity, but a playable rule system or a finite simulation specification is ABSENT. Finite visual samples cannot establish the infinite tiling conclusions. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finite searches are described, but a general-purpose tool contract, input format and practical resource bound are ABSENT. The existence proof alone does not establish a usable tiling generator. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The construction reduces selection of its finite data to terminating finite searches. This supports exact enumeration of admissible primes, colorings and residue transversals, provided candidates are checked against the stated finite predicates. A practical complexity bound is ABSENT. |
| data_structures | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Finite sets, indexed shift lists and labelled blocks give exact representations for checking the construction. In particular, repeated shift values must retain distinct indices even though the tile itself is an ordinary set. This is a representation contract for these finite gadgets; a general data structure performance theorem is ABSENT. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finite arithmetic in the construction does not establish an accelerator architecture or parallel execution contract. A correspondence between hardware behaviour and exact translational tilings is ABSENT. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The arithmetic obstruction excludes fully periodic tilings; a computational hardness assumption or security guarantee is ABSENT. Nonperiodicity alone does not supply a cryptographic primitive. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem concerns exact coverage by translates and full periodicity. A statistical learning model, loss function or approximation guarantee is ABSENT. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The finite selections establish existence of suitable tile data, without an optimisation objective or practical search bound. A correspondence with a scheduling or resource allocation instance is ABSENT. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A finite-index period subgroup describes invariance of a translation set. A protocol, failure model or correspondence with distributed scheduling is ABSENT. |
| other (Translational tiling theory) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The constructed finite tile and its unit-cube thickening admit tilings while excluding full periodicity. The result directly obstructs any universal implication from translational tileability to existence of a fully periodic tiling in the stated discrete and Euclidean settings. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-periodicity** (counterexample_scope): The result excludes full periodicity; it does not assert that every individual nonzero period is absent or that the tile is connected. Blocked use: ['Inferring absence of every individual translational period.', 'Requiring a connected tile without additional evidence.']
- **L-existential** (counterexample_scope): The counterexample concerns the constructed tile. It does not establish nonperiodicity for an arbitrary tile or engineering arrangement. Blocked use: ['Declaring a particular schedule or layout nonperiodic merely because it admits a tiling.']
- **L-common** (hypothesis): All simultaneous tiling equations must use the same unknown translation set. Separate solutions do not establish the common-solution conclusion. Blocked use: ['Replacing a common solution by independently chosen solutions.']
- **L-word** (hypothesis): The vertical-period obstruction requires the line rule and nonconstancy of every column. Blocked use: ['Using the obstruction for arbitrary arrays or arrays with constant columns.']
- **L-real** (edge_case): Integer vertices alone do not justify restricting an arbitrary Euclidean tiling to integer translations. Blocked use: ['Discarding real translation vectors solely because tile vertices are integral.']
- **L-grid** (hypothesis): The grid-center conclusion assumes lattice periodicity of the assembly together with its types. Blocked use: ['Applying the grid-center conclusion to an arbitrary nonperiodic assembly.']
- **L-null** (edge_case): The Euclidean existence conclusion is a tiling up to null sets. Blocked use: ['Demanding disjoint closed boundaries as a consequence of the Euclidean tiling statement.']
- **L-finite** (formal_scope): Terminating finite searches select the tile data. They do not enumerate the infinite translation set that proves existence. Blocked use: ['Treating a finite tile-selection search as an exhaustive enumeration of an infinite tiling.']
- **L-practical** (unknown): A practical size bound or feasible implementation cost for the full finite construction is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Promising practical construction time or memory from finite search termination alone.']
- **L-local** (formal_scope): Finite tests of a local inverse map can check that map on the executed inputs, but cannot certify the infinite tiling and nonperiodicity conclusions. Blocked use: ['Promoting finite local test coverage to a proof of the whole theorem.']
- **L-planar** (counterexample_scope): The positive planar results cited in the introduction do not establish the conjecture for arbitrary bounded measurable Euclidean tiles. Blocked use: ['Extending a rational polygonal result to all bounded measurable Euclidean tiles.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 155 (`lean/docs/155.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A translational tile with no fully periodic tiling in dimension three](../../preprints/A-translational-tile-with-no-fully-periodic-tiling-in-dimension-three-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The periodic-tiling question asks whether a finite tile that tiles a lattice must admit a periodic tiling. The formalized counterexample is a finite tile in $\mathbb Z^3$ that tiles but has no complement invariant under a finite-index subgroup. Its unit-cube thickening also tiles $\mathbb R^3$ almost everywhere but admits no fully periodic tiling, even with arbitrary real translation vectors. The result also establishes that dimension three is the least lattice dimension where this failure occurs.

Comparator source `PeriodicTilingThree.lean` (1994 bytes, sha256 `056c498ce4a6e7b71b688eb8c499ebef70ad1bddfea98b7215d480eb11e08bba`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

universe u

namespace PeriodicTilingThree

abbrev Lattice (d : ℕ) := Fin d → ℤ

variable {G : Type u} [AddCommGroup G]

def Tiles (F : Finset G) (A : Set G) : Prop :=
  Function.Bijective (fun p : ↥F × A => (p.1 : G) + (p.2 : G))

def Period (A : Set G) (v : G) : Prop :=
  ∀ x : G, x + v ∈ A ↔ x ∈ A

def FullyPeriodic (A : Set G) : Prop :=
  ∃ P : AddSubgroup G, P.FiniteIndex ∧ ∀ v ∈ P, Period A v

open MeasureTheory

abbrev Space (d : ℕ) := Fin d → ℝ

def castLattice {d : ℕ} : Lattice d →+ Space d where
  toFun z i := (z i : ℝ)
  map_zero' := by ext i; simp
  map_add' z w := by ext i; simp

def unitCube (d : ℕ) : Set (Space d) := Set.Icc 0 1

def Thickening {d : ℕ} (F : Finset (Lattice d)) : Set (Space d) :=
  {x | ∃ z ∈ F, x - castLattice z ∈ unitCube d}

def AETiles {d : ℕ} (Ω A : Set (Space d)) : Prop :=
  ∀ᵐ x ∂volume, ∃! a : A, x - (a : Space d) ∈ Ω

def latticeOfBasis {d : ℕ} (b : Module.Basis (Fin d) ℝ (Space d)) :
    AddSubgroup (Space d) := (Submodule.span ℤ (Set.range b)).toAddSubgroup

def EuclideanFullyPeriodic {d : ℕ} (A : Set (Space d)) : Prop :=
  ∃ b : Module.Basis (Fin d) ℝ (Space d), ∀ v ∈ latticeOfBasis b, Period A v

def HasTileWithoutPeriodicComplement (d : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ T : Finset (Lattice d), T.Nonempty ∧
    (∃ A : Set (Lattice d), Tiles T A) ∧
    ∀ A : Set (Lattice d), Tiles T A → ¬ FullyPeriodic A

theorem periodic_tiling_counterexample_and_minimality :
    (∃ T : Finset (Lattice 3), T.Nonempty ∧
      (∃ A : Set (Lattice 3), Tiles T A) ∧
      (∀ A : Set (Lattice 3), Tiles T A → ¬ FullyPeriodic A) ∧
      (∃ A : Set (Space 3), AETiles (Thickening T) A) ∧
      (∀ A : Set (Space 3),
        AETiles (Thickening T) A → ¬ EuclideanFullyPeriodic A)) ∧
    IsLeast {d : ℕ | HasTileWithoutPeriodicComplement d} 3 := by
  sorry

end PeriodicTilingThree

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct a finite translational tile in ℤ<sup>3</sup> that admits tilings but no fully periodic tiling. Its unit-cube thickening has the same property in ℝ<sup>3</sup>, even when arbitrary real translations are allowed. This gives a negative resolution of the periodic tiling conjecture in dimension three.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For subsets $A,F$ of an abelian group $G$, write $A\oplus F=G$ when every element of $G$ has a unique expression $a+f$ with $a\in A$ and $f\in F$. A finite nonempty set $F$ is a *translational tile* if such a set $A$ exists. A tiling is *fully periodic* if $A$ is invariant under a subgroup of finite index in $G$. In $\mathbb R^3$, a bounded measurable set of positive measure tiles by translations when its translates partition space up to null sets; full periodicity means that its translation set is invariant under a lattice of full rank. Throughout this paper, “periodic” means fully periodic.

The periodic tiling conjecture asks whether every translational tile admits a periodic tiling. An explicit Euclidean formulation appears in Lagarias and Wang (1996, 346) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[LagariasWang](#ref-LagariasWang)]. Our conclusion in dimension three is the following.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a finite nonempty set $T\subset\mathbb Z^3$ such that:*

1.  *$T$ tiles $\mathbb Z^3$ by translations, but no translation set $A$ with $A\oplus T=\mathbb Z^3$ is invariant under a subgroup of finite index in $\mathbb Z^3$;*

2.  *the set $\Omega=T+[0,1]^3$ tiles $\mathbb R^3$ by translations up to null sets, but no such tiling has a translation set invariant under a lattice of full rank in $\mathbb R^3$.*

</div>

The distinction between a tile and a particular tiling is essential: a tile may admit both periodic and nonperiodic translation sets. The theorem excludes every periodic translation set for the constructed tile. For lattice tilings, dimension three is the smallest possible dimension for this conclusion. Newman’s finite-state argument shows that the translation set of every tiling of $\mathbb Z$ by a finite tile is periodic (Newman 1977, proof of Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Newman](#ref-Newman)]. Bhattacharya proved that every finite tile of $\mathbb Z^2$ admits a periodic tiling (Bhattacharya 2020, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Bhattacharya](#ref-Bhattacharya)]. Greenfeld and Tao subsequently gave a quantitative version of the planar result and proved that the translation set of every tiling of $\mathbb Z^2$ by one finite tile is a disjoint union of finitely many sets, each invariant under some nonzero translation (Greenfeld and Tao 2021, Theorems 1.3<span class="upright">(i)</span> and 1.5) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[GTStructure](#ref-GTStructure)]. An earlier planar result of Wijshoff and Leeuwen (1984, Theorem 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[WijshoffVanLeeuwen](#ref-WijshoffVanLeeuwen)] concerns polyominoes without holes and produces a tiling whose translation set is a single lattice. The unrestricted finite-tile theorem allows arbitrary disconnected sets and only asks for a finite-index period subgroup. For Euclidean tilings, Dios Pont et al. (2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[RationalPolygons](#ref-RationalPolygons)] prove periodic-tiling existence for rational polygonal sets, including disconnected sets and sets with holes. These positive planar results do not establish the conjecture for arbitrary bounded measurable Euclidean tiles. We use the lattice results only for the least-dimension conclusion above; the Euclidean assertion of <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is proved directly below.

Greenfeld and Tao (2024) disproved the periodic tiling conjecture in sufficiently large dimension. Their construction first produces a tile in $\mathbb Z^2\times G_0$, with $G_0$ a finite abelian $2$-group, by encoding a Sudoku rule with an arithmetic obstruction to periodicity. The present proof follows this general strategy and the $p$-adic Sudoku formulation of Greenfeld and Tao (2025, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GTUndecidable](#ref-GTUndecidable)]. We also use the idea of combining simultaneous tiling equations into one equation from Greenfeld and Tao (2024, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[GTPeriodic](#ref-GTPeriodic)]. The quotient-to-lattice reduction of Meyerovitch, Sanadhya, and Solomon (2025, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[MSSReduction](#ref-MSSReduction)] explains the relevance of controlling the finite factor: $\mathbb Z^2$ times a finite cyclic group is a quotient of $\mathbb Z^3$.

Here the Sudoku object is an array $W(n,m)$ over a finite alphabet, with $n$ in a finite interval $I$ of columns and $m\in\mathbb Z$ indexing rows. A finite set of allowed words prescribes the possible samples $(W(n,dn+e))_{n\in I}$ along every integer-slope line. The arithmetic word rule has two features: one such array exists with every column nonconstant, but no array with those properties has a vertical period. We turn these finite word constraints and column conditions into simultaneous tiling equations.

There are two requirements at each stage of this strategy. The encoding must admit at least one tiling, and every fully periodic tiling of the encoded object must yield a fully periodic solution of the preceding constraints. A system of tiling equations therefore means several equations with the *same* unknown translation set. Separate solutions of the equations would not suffice. The finite group matters as well: a general finite abelian group needs several generators, whereas a cyclic group can be represented by just the third integer coordinate.

The central construction here encodes the Sudoku constraints while keeping that finite factor cyclic. For each word position, a finite-valued function has one input digit designated for each symbol. A symbol is called active when changing that digit can change the function’s value. An arbitrary solution may activate several symbols at one position; the constraints are imposed on every resulting choice of symbols. The explicit solution we construct activates just one. Cycle tests exclude forbidden simultaneous dependencies, and activation tests force at least one symbol at every ordinary position. Two seed channels force distinct values in every column of the resulting Sudoku array. The activation tests use a nonuniform list of output shifts with uniform coordinate counts on a product of two prime-order groups. These counts force activity, while suitable permutations of the remaining coordinates give an explicit common solution to all the equations. Both seed tests use the same auxiliary output function. All finite group factors have pairwise coprime orders, so their product remains cyclic. The horizontal residue pairs needed by the seed tests only index values of one cyclic output coordinate; they do not become an additional group factor.

A stacking construction using a fresh prime then gives one tile in $\mathbb Z^2\times\mathbb Z/Q\mathbb Z$. We supply a direct rigidification argument that passes to $\mathbb Z^3$ and also handles the unit-cube thickening in $\mathbb R^3$. Integer vertices alone would not justify restricting an arbitrary Euclidean tiling to integer translations. Our construction uses two component shapes with the same large common part and one displaced marked cube. The common part forces the component centers in a hypothetical periodic tiling onto a translated grid. Coverage of the marked cubes then forces invariance in the quotient kernel, allowing that tiling to descend. Thus the Euclidean argument includes arbitrary real translation vectors. The result concerns full periodicity; neither connectedness of the tile nor absence of every individual nonzero period is asserted.

records the two directions maintained by these constructions. The proof is organized as follows. [sec:word](#sec:word) proves the Sudoku obstruction and gives its last-nonzero-digit model. [sec:encoding](#sec:encoding) constructs the cyclic group and the dependence tests; <a href="#sec:activation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:activation">4</a> forces activity and rules out periodic common solutions. [sec:model](#sec:model) constructs one common solution explicitly. [sec:stacking](#sec:stacking), [sec:geometry](#sec:geometry) perform stacking and the passage to three dimensions. Every construction and nonperiodicity argument needed for <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is proved in the paper.

<figure id="fig:proof-route">

<figcaption>The two directions of the proof. The construction proceeds to the right, starting from the explicit last-nonzero-digit array in <a href="#word:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:model">6</a>. To exclude full periodicity, any hypothetical periodic tiling is decoded in the reverse direction, producing a line-rule array with nonconstant columns and a vertical period. Every tiling equation at the second stage uses the same translation set, and the last stage uses the same $T$ in both the discrete and Euclidean assertions.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="sec:word"></a>
### A word rule with no vertical period

We begin with a finite word constraint whose solutions on an infinite strip cannot have a vertical period if every column is nonconstant. The constraint is the two-digit $p$-adic Sudoku rule of Greenfeld and Tao (2025, Definition 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[GTUndecidable](#ref-GTUndecidable)]. The affine-square and rescaling arguments below follow the framework of Greenfeld and Tao (2025, Theorem 4.7, Lemma 4.8, and Section 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GTUndecidable](#ref-GTUndecidable)]; we give the complete argument for the particular obstruction needed here.

Fix a prime $p>200$, and set $$N=p^2,\qquad \Sigma=\mathbb F_p^\times,
    \qquad I=\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}.$$ When an integer occurs in an expression over $\mathbb F_p$, its residue modulo $p$ is understood.

<a id="stmt-word-definition"></a>
<div id="word:definition" class="definition">

**Definition 2** (Allowed words and the line rule). A word $w\colon I\to\Sigma$ is *allowed* if there are integers $a,b$, not both divisible by $p$, such that, for every $n\in I$, $$w(n)=
 \begin{cases}
   an+b\pmod p, & p\nmid an+b,\\[2pt]
   (an+b)/p\pmod p, & p\mid an+b\text{ and }p^2\nmid an+b.
 \end{cases}$$ At indices with $p^2\mid an+b$, the value may be any member of $\Sigma$. An array $W\colon I\times\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$ satisfies the *line rule* if $$n\longmapsto W(n,dn+e)$$ is an allowed word for every $d,e\in\mathbb Z$. A *positive vertical period* of $W$ is an integer $M>0$ such that $W(n,m+M)=W(n,m)$ for all $(n,m)\in I\times\mathbb Z$.

</div>

The allowed-word constraint depends only on the residues of $a,b$ modulo $p^2$. Indeed, these residues determine the divisibility alternatives and each of the prescribed values. In particular, it is a finite constraint on words over the finite alphabet $\Sigma$.

For every allowed word, reduction of a witnessing pair gives a nonzero affine form $\ell(n)=\bar a n+\bar b$ over $\mathbb F_p$. The word agrees with $\ell$ wherever $\ell\ne0$. A nonzero affine form has at most one zero residue class, so there are at most $N/p=p$ exceptional indices on $I$. Here and below, “nonzero affine form” means that its coefficients are not all zero; a nonzero constant form is permitted.

<a id="stmt-word-affine"></a>
<div id="word:affine" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Global affine approximation). *If $W$ satisfies the line rule, there are $A,B,C\in\mathbb F_p$, not all zero, such that $$W(n,m)=An+Bm+C
    \quad\text{whenever }An+Bm+C\ne0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-word-affine"><summary>Proof of stmt-word-affine</summary>

*Proof.* For each line $m=dn+e$ with $d\in\{-1,0,1\}$, choose the nonzero affine form $\ell_{d,e}(n)$ supplied by an allowed-word witness. Call an index on that line exceptional when $\ell_{d,e}(n)=0$.

First we find a consecutive $4\times4$ square containing no exceptional cell for any of these three line directions. In the rectangle $I\times\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$ there are $(N-3)(K-3)$ such squares. The numbers of lines of slopes $0,1,-1$ meeting the rectangle are respectively $K,K+N-1,K+N-1$. Each line has at most $p$ exceptional cells on the whole strip, and each cell belongs to at most $16$ candidate squares. Consequently at most $$16p(3K+2N-2)\leq16p(3K+2N)$$ squares are excluded. Since $$N-3-48p=p^2-48p-3>0,$$ we can choose an integer $K\geq4$ with $$K>\frac{3(N-3)+32pN}{N-3-48p}.$$ For this choice, $$(N-3)(K-3)-16p(3K+2N)
      =K(N-3-48p)-3(N-3)-32pN>0.$$ A square of the required kind therefore exists.

Write its column indices as $r,r+1,r+2,r+3$ and its row indices as $q,q+1,q+2,q+3$. Each row agrees on the square with an affine function $U_m n+V_m$. For $m=q+1,q+2$ and $n=r+1,r+2$, the second difference along either diagonal vanishes. Thus, for each sign, $$\begin{split}
  0={}&n(U_{m+1}-2U_m+U_{m-1})
       +(V_{m+1}-2V_m+V_{m-1})\\
     &\qquad\qquad{}\pm(U_{m+1}-U_{m-1}).
 \end{split}$$ Subtracting the identities at the two consecutive values of $n$ gives $U_{m+1}-2U_m+U_{m-1}=0$. Subtracting the two signs gives $U_{m+1}=U_{m-1}$, because $2\ne0$ in $\mathbb F_p$. Together these imply $U_{m-1}=U_m=U_{m+1}$. The two overlapping triples show that all four row slopes have a common value $A$. The remaining identities say that the four intercepts have zero second differences, so they have the form $V_m=Bm+C$. Therefore the form $$H(n,m)=An+Bm+C$$ agrees with $W$ on the square. It is not the zero form, because every entry of $W$ lies in $\Sigma$.

Call a cell *good* if either $H(n,m)=0$ or $W(n,m)=H(n,m)$. We claim that four consecutive good cells on a line of slope $0,1$, or $-1$ make the entire line good. The restriction of $H$ to that line is an affine form in $n$. If it is identically zero, the claim follows from the definition of goodness. Otherwise, it and the chosen form $\ell_{d,e}$ each have at most one zero among the four indices, which are distinct modulo $p$. At least two of the four indices remain where both forms are nonzero. At these indices, goodness and the word rule identify both forms with $W$. Two distinct residues determine an affine form, so the two forms are equal. Wherever this common form is nonzero the word rule gives $W=H$, and its zeros are good by definition. This proves the claim.

Applying the claim to the four square rows makes those entire rows good. To adjoin a row immediately above a block of four good rows, consider its cell in column $n$. At least one of $$n+4\leq N-1\qquad\text{or}\qquad n-4\geq0$$ holds, since $N\geq8$. Choose $\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}$ so that the four columns $n+\varepsilon,n+2\varepsilon,n+3\varepsilon,n+4\varepsilon$ are in $I$. If the new row has index $m$, the cells $$(n+j\varepsilon,m-j),\qquad 1\leq j\leq4,$$ are four consecutive good cells on a diagonal through $(n,m)$. The claim makes $(n,m)$ good. For a row immediately below a block of four good rows, use $(n+j\varepsilon,m+j)$ instead. These formulas include both edge columns of the strip. Successively adjoining rows in both directions makes every cell good, proving the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-word-vertical"></a>
<div id="word:vertical" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Vertical nondegeneracy). *Let $H(n,m)=An+Bm+C$ be any affine form supplied by <a href="#word:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:affine">3</a>. Every column of $W$ is nonconstant if and only if $B\ne0$. In that case every positive vertical period of $W$ is divisible by $p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-word-vertical"><summary>Proof of stmt-word-vertical</summary>

*Proof.* If $B=0$, then $An+C$ is not identically zero. Some $n\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$ therefore has $An+C\ne0$, and the entire column at that $n$ has the constant value $An+C$. Conversely, if $B\ne0$, then for any fixed $n$ the form $An+Bm+C$ runs through $\mathbb F_p$ as $m$ runs through a complete residue system modulo $p$. At its $p-1$ nonzero values, the entries of $W$ equal those values. Thus every column takes every symbol of $\Sigma$, and in particular is nonconstant.

Suppose now that $M>0$ satisfies $W(n,m+M)=W(n,m)$ for every $(n,m)$. For a fixed $n$, choose $m$ so that both $H(n,m)$ and $H(n,m+M)$ are nonzero. There are at most two excluded residue classes, so this is possible. Equality of the two entries gives $$BM=H(n,m+M)-H(n,m)=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb F_p.$$ Since $B\ne0$, this implies $p\mid M$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-word-obstruction"></a>
<div id="word:obstruction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (The vertical-period obstruction). *There is no array $W\colon I\times\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$ satisfying the line rule, having every column nonconstant, and having a positive vertical period.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-word-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-word-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose otherwise, and choose the smallest positive integer $M$ that occurs as a vertical period of any such array. Choose an array with period $M$ and an affine approximation $An+Bm+C$. By <a href="#word:vertical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:vertical">4</a>, $B\ne0$ and $p\mid M$.

Choose integers $u,v$ with $A+Bu=C+Bv=0$ in $\mathbb F_p$, and replace $W$ by $$\widetilde W(n,m)=W(n,m+un+v).$$ On a line of slope $d$ and intercept $e$, this samples the original line of slope $d+u$ and intercept $e+v$, so the line rule is preserved. Each column is simply translated in its row coordinate; hence column nonconstancy and the set of vertical periods are preserved. The new affine approximation is $Bm$. Rename this normalized array $W$.

Define $$W_1(n,m)=W(n,pm).$$ This array also satisfies the line rule: its line with slope $d$ and intercept $e$ is the old line with slope $pd$ and intercept $pe$. By <a href="#word:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:affine">3</a>, it has a nonzero affine approximation $H_1(n,m)=A_1n+B_1m+C_1$. We next prove $B_1\ne0$, which is the step needed to retain column nonconstancy under this rescaling.

If $B_1=0$, choose $0\leq n_0<p$ with $c=A_1n_0+C_1\ne0$. Apply the word rule for $W$ to the line $m=n-n_0$, and let $a,b$ be a witnessing pair. Away from the at most two zero residue classes of $\bar a n+\bar b$ and $B(n-n_0)$, both forms equal the array entry on that line. Since $p-2\geq2$, they agree at two distinct residues and therefore are the same affine form. In particular, $$a\equiv B\pmod p,\qquad b\equiv-Bn_0\pmod p.$$ It follows that $p\mid an_0+b$ and $p\nmid a$.

For $0\leq k<p$, put $n=n_0+pk$; all these indices lie in $I$, including the largest possible value $p^2-1$. At the corresponding point of the line, the entry is $$W(n_0+pk,pk)=W_1(n_0+pk,k)=c,$$ where the last equality follows from the nonzero value $H_1(n_0+pk,k)=c$. On the other hand, $$a(n_0+pk)+b
       =p\left(\frac{an_0+b}{p}+ak\right).$$ Whenever the parenthesized expression is nonzero modulo $p$, the second case of the allowed-word rule forces this entry to equal $$\frac{an_0+b}{p}+ak\pmod p.$$ As $k$ runs from $0$ to $p-1$, these residues run through all of $\mathbb F_p$, because $p\nmid a$. Thus $p-1$ of the entries are forced to take the $p-1$ distinct nonzero values, contradicting their common value $c$. The remaining index, where the rule prescribes no value, is not needed for this contradiction.

We have proved $B_1\ne0$, so every column of $W_1$ is nonconstant by <a href="#word:vertical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:vertical">4</a>. But $M/p$ is a positive vertical period of $W_1$, since $$W_1(n,m+M/p)=W(n,pm+M)=W(n,pm)=W_1(n,m).$$ This contradicts the choice of $M$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-word-model"></a>
<div id="word:model" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (A model for the line rule). *For $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, let $\operatorname{ord}_p(t)$ be the largest nonnegative integer $r$ for which $p^r\mid t$, and define $$f(t)=\frac{t}{p^{\operatorname{ord}_p(t)}}\pmod p,
    \qquad f(0)=1.$$ Then $f\colon\mathbb Z\to\Sigma$, and the array $W(n,m)=f(m)$ satisfies the line rule and has every column nonconstant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-word-model"><summary>Proof of stmt-word-model</summary>

*Proof.* The definition gives a nonzero residue for every nonzero integer, including negative integers. For every $r\geq0$ and $t\in\mathbb Z$ we have $f(p^rt)=f(t)$; this also holds at $t=0$ by definition.

Fix integers $d,e$. If $(d,e)\ne(0,0)$, let $r$ be the largest integer such that $p^r$ divides both coefficients, and write $d=p^ra$, $e=p^rb$. The pair $a,b$ is not both divisible by $p$, and $$f(dn+e)=f(an+b)\qquad(n\in I).$$ When $an+b$ has $p$-adic order zero or one, this is exactly the respective value prescribed by the allowed-word rule. When $p^2\mid an+b$, including $an+b=0$, the rule leaves the value unrestricted. Thus $a,b$ witness that the word is allowed. If $(d,e)=(0,0)$, the word is constantly $1$ and has witness $a=0,b=1$. Finally $f(1)=1$ and $f(2)=2$, so every column is nonconstant. ◻

</details>

<a id="word:line-maps"></a>
For the encoding that follows, parametrize the lines by $x=(x_1,x_2)\in\mathbb Z^2$ and write $$\label{word:line-maps}
    L_n(x)=x_2+nx_1,\qquad u_n=(1,-n)\qquad(n\in I).$$ The map $L_n\colon\mathbb Z^2\to\mathbb Z$ is surjective and its kernel is generated by $u_n$: the equation $x_2+nx_1=0$ is equivalent to $x=x_1(1,-n)$. The model supplies the allowed word $\bigl(f(L_n(x))\bigr)_{n\in I}$ at every $x$. Moreover, if $x_1\equiv0$ and $x_2\equiv t\pmod p$ for $t\in\{1,2\}$, every entry of this word equals $t$. Indeed, every $L_n(x)$ then has the nonzero residue $t$, so its last nonzero digit is $t$.

<a id="sec:encoding"></a>
### Encoding symbols in a cyclic finite coordinate

We now express the word constraints by finitely many translational tiling equations on a common unknown set. A symbol will be represented by the dependence of a function on one of its input digits. The present section constructs tests forbidding specified simultaneous dependences; the next section will force enough dependence to obtain an array. Encoding functional constraints by tiling equations is developed in Greenfeld and Tao (2023, secs. 4–8) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GTTwoTiles](#ref-GTTwoTiles)] and Greenfeld and Tao (2024, Theorem 4.1). The issue here is to carry out the required tests with a cyclic finite factor; the graph, dependence, and cycle constructions below supply their exact local forms.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Channels and the ambient group

Retain the prime $p>200$, the width $N=p^2$, and the alphabet $\Sigma=\mathbb F_p^\times$ from the preceding section. Recall the line maps $L_n(x)=x_2+nx_1$ and their kernel generators $u_n=(1,-n)$ from <a href="#word:line-maps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="word:line-maps">[word:line-maps]</a>. The set of *channels* is $$\mathcal I=\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\sqcup\{\eta_1,\eta_2\}.$$ The first $N$ channels are ordinary; the last two are seeds. Their input digit labels are $J_n=\Sigma$ and $J_{\eta_t}=\{*\}$, respectively. The seeds will eventually require the ordinary channels to display the constant words $1$ and $2$ at some points. Set $$S=(\mathbb Z/p^2\mathbb Z)^2,\qquad s(x)=x\bmod p^2,\qquad D=\abs{S}=p^4.$$ Here $S$ records residues of the horizontal variable $x$.

For each $i\in\mathcal I$, choose primes $a_i,b_i$ and put $P_i=\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z\times\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z$. Require $$a_{\eta_1}=2,\qquad a_{\eta_2}=3,\qquad
 b_{\eta_1},b_{\eta_2}>D,$$ and choose all the $a_i,b_i$ to be distinct and different from $p$. For each $j\in J_i$, choose a further prime $r_{ij}$, with all these primes distinct and disjoint from the previous choices, such that <a id="enc:digit-partition"></a>$$r_{ij}=A_{ij}a_i+B_{ij}b_i,
 \qquad A_{ij},B_{ij}\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}.
 \label{enc:digit-partition}$$ These choices are possible using only finitely many primes. Indeed, if $a,b$ are coprime and $r\geq(a-1)b$, choose $B\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$ with $Bb\equiv r\pmod a$; then $A=(r-Bb)/a$ is a nonnegative integer. There are arbitrarily large primes outside any prescribed finite set, so this argument applies successively to every required $r_{ij}$.

<a id="enc:ambient"></a>
Write $$r_i=\prod_{j\in J_i}r_{ij},\qquad
 K_i=\mathbb Z/r_i\mathbb Z\cong\prod_{j\in J_i}\mathbb Z/r_{ij}\mathbb Z,\qquad
 K=\prod_{i\in\mathcal I}K_i,\qquad
 P=\prod_{i\in\mathcal I}P_i.$$ The displayed identification of $K_i$ is the Chinese remainder isomorphism, given by taking residues. Let $U_{ij}\leq K_i$ be the coordinate subgroup supported on the digit $j$ in this identification. We work in $$G=\mathbb Z^2\times V,\qquad
 V=\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal I}
       \bigl(\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z\times P_i\bigr).
 \label{enc:ambient}$$ The group $V$ is cyclic. To see this, expand each $P_i$ into its two cyclic factors. The orders $D$, $r_i^2$, $a_i$, and $b_i$ of all the resulting factors are pairwise coprime. A tuple of generators therefore has order equal to their product, which is $\abs V$. The residue set $S$ is not an additional factor of $V$; the coordinate of order $D$ in <a href="#enc:ambient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:ambient">[enc:ambient]</a> is the cyclic group $\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z$.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### One graph and freely chosen high coordinates

<a id="enc:graph"></a>
Write an element of $G$ as $$\bigl(x,z,(v_i,c_i)_{i\in\mathcal I}\bigr),\qquad
 z\in\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z,\quad v_i\in\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z,\quad c_i\in P_i.$$ Its low coordinates are $k_i=v_i\bmod r_i\in K_i$. Let $q_0:G\to\mathbb Z^2\times K$ be the homomorphism retaining just $(x,k)$, and let $$H_0=\ker q_0
   =\{0\}\times\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z\times
       \prod_{i\in\mathcal I}
       \bigl(r_i(\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z)\times P_i\bigr).$$ Include the tiling equation $$A\oplus H_0=G.
 \label{enc:graph}$$ For a target point in any fibre of $q_0$, every element of $A$ in that fibre supplies exactly one representation with a summand in $H_0$. Consequently <a href="#enc:graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:graph">[enc:graph]</a> says precisely that $A$ contains one point above each $(x,k)$. We may thus denote its outputs by $c_i(x,k)$, $z(x,k)$, and $v_i(x,k)$.

<a id="enc:shear"></a>
For subsequent constructions it is useful to describe $v_i$ by a sheared high coordinate $\beta_i(x,k)\in K_i$, defined by $$v_i(x,k)=x_1+[k_i-x_1]_{r_i}+r_i\beta_i(x,k)
       \quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z.
 \label{enc:shear}$$ Here $[\cdot]_{r_i}$ is the least nonnegative representative modulo $r_i$. For fixed $x,k_i$, the right side is a bijection from $K_i$ onto the elements of $\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z$ with residue $k_i$. Thus arbitrary functions $c_i$, $\beta_i$, and $z$ specify exactly one graph satisfying <a href="#enc:graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:graph">[enc:graph]</a>. This is a choice of coordinates on each fibre; it does not assert a direct-product splitting of $\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z$ into two groups of order $r_i$.

If we add $h=(h_1,h_2)$ to $x$ and $h_1$ to $v_i$, the new low coordinate is $k_i+h_1$. Since $$[(k_i+h_1)-(x_1+h_1)]_{r_i}=[k_i-x_1]_{r_i},$$ this operation preserves $\beta_i$. The identity holds for every integer lift of $h_1$, including negative ones.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Dependence constraints on the useful outputs

<a id="enc:dependence"></a>
We require exactly the following restrictions on the functions $c_i$: $$\begin{aligned}
 c_n(x,k)&=\widetilde c_n(L_n(x),k_n)
       &&(0\leq n<N),\\
 c_{\eta_t}(x,k)&=\widetilde c_{\eta_t}(s(x),k_{\eta_t})
       &&(t=1,2).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{enc:dependence}$$ The outputs $c_i$ will encode symbols; the other outputs will be used to satisfy the activation equations.

<a id="stmt-enc-invariance"></a>
<div id="enc:invariance" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *Fix a channel $i$ and a shift $\sigma\in\mathbb Z^2\times K$. There is a finite nonempty tile $F_{i,\sigma}\subset G$ such that, for every graph satisfying <a href="#enc:graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:graph">[enc:graph]</a>, $$A\oplus F_{i,\sigma}=G
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 c_i(b-\sigma)=c_i(b)\text{ for every }b\in\mathbb Z^2\times K.$$ This equation imposes no restriction on the graph’s other outputs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-enc-invariance"><summary>Proof of stmt-enc-invariance</summary>

*Proof.* Choose any lift $g\in G$ of $\sigma$ with $c_i$-coordinate zero, and partition $H_0$ into $$E_0^{(i)}=\{h\in H_0:c_i(h)=0\},\qquad
 E_{\ne0}^{(i)}=H_0\setminus E_0^{(i)}.$$ Set $$F_{i,\sigma}=E_{\ne0}^{(i)}\sqcup(g+E_0^{(i)}).$$ The union is disjoint because its two parts have, respectively, nonzero and zero $c_i$-coordinates. Fix a target base $b$. The unique graph point above $b$, together with $E_{\ne0}^{(i)}$, covers exactly those points in that fibre whose $c_i$-coordinate differs from $c_i(b)$, once each. The graph point above $b-\sigma$, together with $g+E_0^{(i)}$, covers exactly the points whose $c_i$-coordinate is $c_i(b-\sigma)$, once each. In both assertions, every choice of the other outputs and high coordinates is attained exactly once, since those offsets range freely in $E_0^{(i)}$. The two contributions partition the target fibre precisely when the displayed equality of useful outputs holds. ◻

</details>

Apply <a href="#enc:invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="enc:invariance">7</a>, for each $i$, to a generator of every $K_\ell$ with $\ell\ne i$, keeping all other base coordinates fixed. This removes dependence on the other low inputs. For an ordinary channel $n$, also apply it to the horizontal shift $u_n$ with zero low-coordinate shift. Since $\ker L_n=\mathbb Zu_n$, the remaining dependence on $x$ factors through $L_n(x)$. For each seed use instead the two horizontal shifts $(p^2,0)$ and $(0,p^2)$, again with zero low-coordinate shift. These give precisely dependence on $s(x)$. There are only finitely many such generators and tiles, and their equations are equivalent to <a href="#enc:dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:dependence">[enc:dependence]</a>. In particular, they leave all the functions $\beta_i$ and $z$ unrestricted.

For a graph with these dependences, write $g_{i,x}:K_i\to P_i$ for its useful output at $x$. Define its *active labels* by $$\mathcal A_i(x)=
 \bigl\{j\in J_i:\text{there exist }w,w'\in K_i
   \text{ with }w-w'\in U_{ij}
   \text{ and }g_{i,x}(w)\ne g_{i,x}(w')\bigr\}.$$ Thus a label is inactive exactly when changing that digit never changes the output. For ordinary $n$, the set $\mathcal A_n(x)$ depends only on $L_n(x)$; for a seed it depends only on $s(x)$. A function independent of every digit is constant, since one can pass between any two inputs by changing one digit at a time. At this stage the active sets are allowed to be empty.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### A cycle test for simultaneous activity

<a id="stmt-enc-exclusion"></a>
<div id="enc:exclusion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Exclusion of simultaneous activity). *Let $C=(i_1,\ldots,i_t)$ be a nonempty cyclically ordered list of distinct channels, and choose a label $j(i)\in J_i$ for each $i\in C$. There is a finite family of finite nonempty tiles such that a graph satisfying <a href="#enc:dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:dependence">[enc:dependence]</a> tiles with every member of this family if and only if, at every $x\in\mathbb Z^2$, at least one of the chosen labels $j(i)$ is inactive.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-enc-exclusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-enc-exclusion</summary>

<a id="enc:cycle-tile"></a>
*Proof.* For each $i\in C$, let $\operatorname{prev}(i)$ denote the preceding channel in the cycle. Choose arbitrary maps $$d_i:P_{\operatorname{prev}(i)}\longrightarrow U_{i,j(i)}.$$ For $e=(e_i)_{i\in\mathcal I}\in P$, put $$d_i[e]=
 \begin{cases}
  d_i(e_{\operatorname{prev}(i)})&i\in C,\\
  0&i\notin C.
 \end{cases}$$ Include a tile for every joint choice $d=(d_i)_{i\in C}$, namely $$F_d=\left\{
 \bigl(0,\zeta,(\upsilon_i,e_i)_{i\in\mathcal I}\bigr):
 \begin{array}{l}
 e\in P,\quad \zeta\in\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z,\\
 \upsilon_i\in\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z,\quad
 \upsilon_i\bmod r_i=d_i[e]\quad(i\in\mathcal I)
 \end{array}
 \right\}.
 \label{enc:cycle-tile}$$ This is an ordinary set: distinct $e$ give distinct useful-output offsets, and every choice of $\zeta$ and the lifts $\upsilon_i$ occurs once. Both the tile and the family of all maps $d$ are finite.

<a id="enc:permutation"></a>
Fix a target base $(x,k)$. For a fixed $e$, a representation using <a href="#enc:cycle-tile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:cycle-tile">[enc:cycle-tile]</a> must start at the graph point over $(x,(k_i-d_i[e])_i)$. The useful outputs of the target are then $$\Phi_{x,k,d}(e)
   =\bigl(e_i+g_{i,x}(k_i-d_i[e])\bigr)_{i\in\mathcal I}.
 \label{enc:permutation}$$ For any prescribed remaining coordinates of the target, the offsets $\zeta$ and $\upsilon_i$ are uniquely determined: each low residue is already correct, and all its lifts occur in the tile. Hence $A\oplus F_d=G$ holds exactly when <a href="#enc:permutation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:permutation">[enc:permutation]</a> is a permutation of $P$ for every $(x,k)$. This criterion is independent of the graph’s high outputs and its $z$-output.

Suppose some $j(i_0)$ is inactive at $x$. Then the $i_0$-component of <a href="#enc:permutation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:permutation">[enc:permutation]</a> is $e_{i_0}+g_{i_0,x}(k_{i_0})$, independent of the preceding component of $e$. From any target useful-output tuple, we can therefore recover $e_{i_0}$ uniquely. The next equation around the cycle then recovers the next component, and continuing recovers all components on $C$. The starting equation remains satisfied because it never depended on the preceding component. Off the cycle, each equation is just a fixed translation and recovers its component separately. This gives a unique preimage for every target, for all $k$ and all maps $d$.

Conversely, suppose every chosen label is active at some $x$. For each $i\in C$ choose $w_i,w_i'\in K_i$ such that $$w_i-w_i'\in U_{i,j(i)},\qquad
 \varepsilon_i:=g_{i,x}(w_i)-g_{i,x}(w_i')\ne0.$$ Set $k_i=w_i$ on the cycle, and choose the other $k_i$ arbitrarily. Extend $\varepsilon$ by zero off $C$ to an element of $P$. For each $i\in C$, choose a map with $$d_i(0)=0,\qquad
 d_i(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{prev}(i)})=w_i-w_i'.$$ The two specified arguments are distinct, so these prescriptions extend to maps on the whole domain. They are among the maps tested in the finite family. At this base, the distinct inputs $0$ and $\varepsilon$ have the same image under <a href="#enc:permutation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:permutation">[enc:permutation]</a>: on $C$ their outputs are respectively $g_{i,x}(w_i)$ and $\varepsilon_i+g_{i,x}(w_i')$, and off $C$ they coincide as well. The permutation condition fails. This proves both directions. ◻

</details>

Let $\mathcal W\subseteq\Sigma^N$ be the finite set of allowed words from the word rule. We impose the following specific instances of <a href="#enc:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="enc:exclusion">8</a>:

1.  For every $w\in\Sigma^N\setminus\mathcal W$, use the cycle $(0,1,\ldots,N-1)$ with label $j(n)=w(n)$.

2.  For each $t\in\{1,2\}$, each ordinary channel $n$, and each $j\in\Sigma\setminus\{t\}$, use the two-channel cycle $(\eta_t,n)$ with labels $*$ and $j$.

All these instances form a finite family. Their precise consequences are <a id="enc:word-exclusions"></a>$$\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathcal A_n(x)\subseteq\mathcal W,
 \qquad
 *\in\mathcal A_{\eta_t}(x)
   \ \Longrightarrow\ 
   \mathcal A_n(x)\subseteq\{t\}\quad(0\leq n<N).
 \label{enc:word-exclusions}$$ The first product is identified with a set of words in the natural order of the channels. These statements also hold when some active sets are empty. The activation tiles constructed next will ensure that every ordinary active set is nonempty at every point and that each seed is active somewhere.

Every tile constructed in this section is a finite nonempty subset of $G$. As a check on the fibre counts, all have cardinality $$\abs{H_0}=D\prod_{i\in\mathcal I}r_i\abs{P_i}:$$ the dependence tiles partition a copy of $H_0$ into two shifted pieces, while each cycle tile has $\abs P$ choices of $e$, $D$ choices of $\zeta$, and $r_i$ choices of each lift $\upsilon_i$.

<a id="sec:activation"></a>
### Forcing activity and excluding full periodicity

The exclusion equations constrain which labels can be active together. We now add one finite tile for each channel to ensure that every ordinary channel is active at every point, and that each seed is active somewhere. Throughout this section, we retain the groups and coordinates of <a href="#sec:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:encoding">3</a>. In particular, the useful output of channel $i$ is $c_i\in P_i=\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z\times\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z$, its low input is $k_i\in K_i$, and its sheared high coordinate is $\beta_i\in K_i$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### A shift list with two fibre orderings

The shift list will serve two purposes: its nonuniform multiplicities will force activity, while its uniform counts in each coordinate will permit the explicit common solution in <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">5</a>.

Fix a channel $i$. For $e\in P_i$, let $\mathbf 1_{\{e\}}$ denote the indicator of the singleton $\{e\}$, and define the integer-valued functions $$\omega_i
 =\mathbf 1_{\{(0,0)\}}-\mathbf 1_{\{(1,0)\}}
  -\mathbf 1_{\{(0,1)\}}+\mathbf 1_{\{(1,1)\}},
 \qquad
 \mu_i=1+\omega_i
 \quad\text{on }P_i.$$ Here $1$ denotes the constant function. The function $\mu_i$ has values two at $(0,0)$ and $(1,1)$, zero at $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$, and one elsewhere. It is therefore a nonnegative, nonconstant multiplicity function. Its total mass is $a_i b_i$, and its marginals are uniform: $$\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z}\mu_i(\alpha,\xi)=a_i
 \quad(\xi\in\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z),
 \qquad
 \sum_{\xi\in\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z}\mu_i(\alpha,\xi)=b_i
 \quad(\alpha\in\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z).$$ Choose an index set $\Delta_i$ of size $a_i b_i$ and a list $e_\delta=(e_\delta^a,e_\delta^b)\in P_i$, $\delta\in\Delta_i$, in which each $e\in P_i$ occurs $\mu_i(e)$ times. The following notation for the list and its orderings is local to channel $i$.

<a id="act:fibre-orderings"></a>
For each $\xi$, order the $a_i$ indices with $e_\delta^b=\xi$ by an arbitrary bijection to $\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z$, and define $\rho^a(\delta)$ by subtracting $e_\delta^a$ from that assigned value. Independently, for each $\alpha$, order the $b_i$ indices with $e_\delta^a=\alpha$ by a bijection to $\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z$, and subtract $e_\delta^b$ to define $\rho^b(\delta)$. Thus both maps in $$\label{act:fibre-orderings}
\begin{aligned}
 \{\delta\in\Delta_i:e_\delta^b=\xi\}
   &\longrightarrow\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z,
 &\delta&\longmapsto e_\delta^a+\rho^a(\delta),\\
 \{\delta\in\Delta_i:e_\delta^a=\alpha\}
   &\longrightarrow\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z,
 &\delta&\longmapsto e_\delta^b+\rho^b(\delta)
\end{aligned}$$ are bijections. Repeated values of $e_\delta$ have distinct indices; the orderings act on these indices.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The activation tiles

For a channel $\ell$, write $R_\ell=r_\ell\mathbb Z/r_\ell^2\mathbb Z$ for the subgroup of offsets with low coordinate zero. It has $r_\ell$ elements. Given $h\in\mathbb Z^2$, $e\in P_i$, and $E\subseteq\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z$, define a batch of offsets by $$\begin{split}
 \mathcal B_i(h,e;E)=
 \bigl\{\bigl(h,\zeta,(w_\ell,d_\ell)_{\ell\in\mathcal I}\bigr):
 &\ \zeta\in E,\quad
 w_i=h_1\pmod{r_i^2},\quad d_i=e,\\[-2pt]
 &\ w_\ell\in R_\ell,\quad d_\ell\in P_\ell
       \text{ for every }\ell\ne i\bigr\}.
\end{split}$$ Every combination of the freely varied coordinates occurs once in this set.

<a id="act:ordinary-crt"></a>
For an ordinary channel $i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}$ and each pair $(l,\delta)\in K_i\times\Delta_i$, choose an integer multiple $h=h^{(i)}_{l,\delta}$ of $u_i=(1,-i)$ such that $$\label{act:ordinary-crt}
 h_1\equiv l\pmod{r_i},\qquad
 h_1\equiv\rho^a(\delta)\pmod{a_i},\qquad
 h_1\equiv\rho^b(\delta)\pmod{b_i}.$$ The moduli are pairwise coprime, so the Chinese remainder theorem gives infinitely many choices of $h_1$. Choose these horizontal offsets distinctly for the different pairs $(l,\delta)$, and set $$F_i^{\mathrm{act}}
 =\bigcup_{(l,\delta)\in K_i\times\Delta_i}
    \mathcal B_i\bigl(h^{(i)}_{l,\delta},e_\delta;\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z\bigr).$$ The restriction $h\in\mathbb Zu_i$ gives $L_i(x-h)=L_i(x)$ for every $x$.

For a seed $i\in\{\eta_1,\eta_2\}$, let $i'$ denote the other seed. For every $(l,\delta,\tau)\in K_i\times\Delta_i\times S$, choose $h=h^{(i)}_{l,\delta,\tau}\in\mathbb Z^2$ satisfying <a href="#act:ordinary-crt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:ordinary-crt">[act:ordinary-crt]</a> and the additional conditions <a id="act:seed-crt"></a>$$\label{act:seed-crt}
 s(h)=\tau,\qquad h_1\equiv0\pmod{a_{i'}}.$$ There is no slope restriction in this case. Writing $\tau=(\tau_1,\tau_2)\in(\mathbb Z/p^2\mathbb Z)^2$, the first coordinate is prescribed modulo the pairwise coprime integers $r_i,a_i,b_i,p^2,a_{i'}$, while the second coordinate only needs to satisfy $h_2\equiv\tau_2\pmod{p^2}$. Again there are infinitely many choices, so take all horizontal offsets within this seed tile to be distinct. Define $$F_i^{\mathrm{act}}
 =\bigcup_{(l,\delta,\tau)\in K_i\times\Delta_i\times S}
    \mathcal B_i\bigl(h^{(i)}_{l,\delta,\tau},e_\delta;\{0\}\bigr).$$ Thus an ordinary batch varies the $z$-offset freely, whereas a seed batch has $z$-offset zero. The congruence involving $a_{i'}$ will allow both seed tests to hold with one common $z$-output in the construction of <a href="#model:system" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:system">14</a>.

All unions just defined are disjoint unions of finite sets, because different batches have different horizontal offsets. In particular, the repeated entries of the auxiliary shift list do not introduce weighted tile elements. These tiles are nonempty, and direct counting gives, for every channel $i$, $$\abs{F_i^{\mathrm{act}}}
   =D\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal I}r_\ell\abs{P_\ell}
   =\abs{H_0}.$$ We add the equations $A\oplus F_i^{\mathrm{act}}=G$ to the graph, dependence, and exclusion equations.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Exact coverage in a fibre

<a id="stmt-act-fibre-criterion"></a>
<div id="act:fibre-criterion" class="lemma">

<a id="act:source-base"></a>
<a id="act:ordinary-map"></a>
<a id="act:seed-map"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Activation fibre criterion). *Suppose $A\oplus H_0=G$, so that $A$ is a graph over $(x,k)$. For a target base point $(x,k)$ and a batch with horizontal offset $h$, write $$\label{act:source-base}
 x'=x-h,\qquad k_i'=k_i-l,\qquad k_\ell'=k_\ell\quad(\ell\ne i).$$ For an ordinary channel $i$, the equation $A\oplus F_i^{\mathrm{act}}=G$ holds if and only if, at every target base point, the map $$\label{act:ordinary-map}
 (l,\delta)\longmapsto
 \bigl(c_i(x',k')+e_\delta,\,\beta_i(x',k')\bigr)$$ is a bijection from $K_i\times\Delta_i$ to $P_i\times K_i$. For a seed $i$, the corresponding necessary and sufficient condition is that, at every target base point, $$\label{act:seed-map}
 (l,\delta,\tau)\longmapsto
 \bigl(c_i(x',k')+e_\delta,\,\beta_i(x',k'),\,z(x',k')\bigr)$$ be a bijection from $K_i\times\Delta_i\times S$ to $P_i\times K_i\times\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-act-fibre-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-act-fibre-criterion</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a target base point and a batch. Every offset in that batch has the same horizontal and low coordinates, so the graph equation supplies exactly one possible source point of $A$: the point above the base in <a href="#act:source-base" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:source-base">[act:source-base]</a>. The $i$th low-coordinate shift is $l$ because $h_1\equiv l\pmod{r_i}$. Since $x'_1=x_1-h_1$, we have $$[k_i'-x'_1]_{r_i}=[k_i-x_1]_{r_i}.$$ Consequently the shear formula <a href="#enc:shear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:shear">[enc:shear]</a> gives $$v_i(x',k')+h_1
   =x_1+[k_i-x_1]_{r_i}+r_i\beta_i(x',k')
   \quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z.$$ Thus the offset preserves the sheared high coordinate $\beta_i$ exactly, and the target useful output is $c_i(x',k')+e_\delta$.

For every $\ell\ne i$, adding the freely chosen element of $R_\ell$ fills the entire high-coordinate fibre with low coordinate $k_\ell$ once. Independently, adding the freely chosen element of $P_\ell$ fills that useful-output coordinate once. In an ordinary batch the $z$-coordinate is also filled once; in a seed batch it remains equal to $z(x',k')$. Hence each batch covers exactly the part of the target fibre specified by its tuple in <a href="#act:ordinary-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:ordinary-map">[act:ordinary-map]</a> or <a href="#act:seed-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:seed-map">[act:seed-map]</a>, with one representation for every choice of the remaining coordinates. Distinct batches correspond to distinct tile elements, so exact coverage by the whole tile is equivalent to every specified tuple occurring once. This is precisely the asserted bijection condition. ◻

</details>

In particular, exact coverage forces every value of the target $c_i$ to occur $r_i$ times among the ordinary batches, or $D r_i$ times among the seed batches. These necessary marginal counts already force the activity we need.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Every required channel is active

<a id="stmt-act-ordinary"></a>
<div id="act:ordinary" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Ordinary activation). *Every ordinary channel has at least one active label at every $x\in\mathbb Z^2$ in any common solution of the equations specified so far.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-act-ordinary"><summary>Proof of stmt-act-ordinary</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose channel $i$ has no active label at some $x$. A function on the finite product $K_i=\prod_{j\in J_i}\mathbb Z/r_{ij}\mathbb Z$ that is independent of every digit is constant: any two inputs can be joined by changing one digit at a time. Thus $g_{i,x}$ has a constant value $c_0\in P_i$. By the dependence condition <a href="#enc:dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:dependence">[enc:dependence]</a> and the equality $L_i(x-h)=L_i(x)$, every source in the activation test at $(x,k)$ has $c_i(x',k')=c_0$. For each $\delta$ there are $r_i$ choices of $l$. The number of batches giving any target value $c\in P_i$ is therefore $$r_i\mu_i(c-c_0).$$ The fibre criterion requires this number to be $r_i$ for every $c$. That is impossible because $\mu_i$ is not constant. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-act-seed"></a>
<div id="act:seed" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Seed activation). *Each of the two seeds has its label active at some point of $\mathbb Z^2$ in any common solution of the equations specified so far.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-act-seed"><summary>Proof of stmt-act-seed</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a seed $i$ and suppose it is nowhere active. The dependence condition and the absence of activity give a function $q:S\to P_i$ such that $c_i(x,k)=q(s(x))$ for every base point. Define its nonnegative integer histogram by $$Q(\alpha,\xi)
 =\abs{\{s\in S:q(s)=(\alpha,\xi)\}},
 \qquad
 \sum_{(\alpha,\xi)\in P_i}Q(\alpha,\xi)=D.$$ At any target base point and for a fixed $\delta$, the source pairs $$(s(x'),k_i')=(s(x)-\tau,k_i-l)$$ run once through $S\times K_i$ as $(\tau,l)$ varies. Thus the number of seed batches with useful output $c\in P_i$ is $r_i\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_i}Q(c-e_\delta)$. The necessary marginal count from <a href="#act:fibre-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:fibre-criterion">9</a>, divided by $r_i$, yields $$(Q*\mu_i)(c)=D\qquad(c\in P_i),$$ where $(Q*\mu_i)(c)=\sum_{e\in P_i}\mu_i(e)Q(c-e)$ is convolution on the finite group $P_i$. Convolution with the constant function $1$ already equals $D$, so $Q*\omega_i=0$. Written out, this says <a id="act:mixed-difference"></a>$$\label{act:mixed-difference}
 Q(\alpha,\xi)-Q(\alpha-1,\xi)
 -Q(\alpha,\xi-1)+Q(\alpha-1,\xi-1)=0.$$

We spell out the integer and positivity consequences of this identity. For each $\alpha$, the difference $Q(\alpha,\xi)-Q(\alpha-1,\xi)$ is independent of $\xi$, because successive differences in the second coordinate vanish. Summing such differences along the cyclic first coordinate shows that, for any $\alpha_0$, $$Q(\alpha,\xi)-Q(\alpha_0,\xi)
   =Q(\alpha,0)-Q(\alpha_0,0).$$ Choose $\alpha_0$ to minimize $Q(\alpha,0)$, and put $$u(\alpha)=Q(\alpha,0)-Q(\alpha_0,0),
 \qquad v(\xi)=Q(\alpha_0,\xi).$$ Both functions take nonnegative integer values, and $Q(\alpha,\xi)=u(\alpha)+v(\xi)$. Summing gives $$D=b_i\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z}u(\alpha)
     +a_i\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z}v(\xi).$$ Since $b_i>D$ and both sums are nonnegative integers, the first sum must vanish. It follows that $a_i$ divides $D=p^4$. But the two seed values of $a_i$ are $2$ and $3$, whereas $p>200$ is prime. This contradiction proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Extraction of the line array

The graph, dependence, exclusion, and activation equations now form a finite system of tiling equations in $G$. Its solvability will be proved in the next section. We can already exclude all fully periodic common solutions.

<a id="stmt-act-nonperiodic"></a>
<div id="act:nonperiodic" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *No common solution of this finite system is invariant under a finite-index subgroup of $G$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-act-nonperiodic"><summary>Proof of stmt-act-nonperiodic</summary>

*Proof.* Let $A$ be any common solution. Fix a total ordering of $\Sigma$. For each ordinary channel $n$ and integer $m$, define $$W(n,m)=\min\mathcal A_n((0,m)),$$ where the minimum is taken in that ordering. This is defined by <a href="#act:ordinary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:ordinary">10</a>. The dependence condition implies that $\mathcal A_n(x)$ depends only on $L_n(x)$; since $L_n(0,m)=m$, the selected symbol at any $x$ is exactly $W(n,L_n(x))$.

For any integers $d,e$, consider $x=(d,e)$. If the word $(W(n,dn+e))_{0\le n<N}$ were forbidden, all its selected labels would be active at $x$. This contradicts the corresponding exclusion equations, by <a href="#enc:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="enc:exclusion">8</a>. Hence $W$ satisfies the line rule.

By <a href="#act:seed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:seed">11</a>, for each $t\in\{1,2\}$ there is a point $x^{(t)}$ where the label of $\eta_t$ is active. The seed-pair exclusions imply that $\mathcal A_n(x^{(t)})$ contains no symbol other than $t$. Since this set is nonempty, it equals $\{t\}$. Therefore $$W\bigl(n,L_n(x^{(1)})\bigr)=1,
 \qquad
 W\bigl(n,L_n(x^{(2)})\bigr)=2
