 \quad\text{for every }0\le n<N.$$ The two arguments in each column must be distinct, and every column of $W$ is nonconstant.

Suppose now that $A$ is invariant under a finite-index subgroup $\Lambda\le G$. Consider the actual group element $$e_{\mathrm v}=\bigl((0,1),0_V\bigr)\in G.$$ Its coset has finite order in $G/\Lambda$, so some integer $M>0$ satisfies $M e_{\mathrm v}\in\Lambda$. We thus obtain a period with *zero finite-coordinate component*: $$A+\bigl((0,M),0_V\bigr)=A.$$ Translation by this element takes the unique graph point above $(x,k)$ to the unique graph point above $(x+(0,M),k)$ and leaves all useful outputs unchanged. It follows that every active-label set is unchanged under $x\mapsto x+(0,M)$. At $x=(0,m)$ this gives $W(n,m+M)=W(n,m)$ for all $n,m$. The array is therefore vertically periodic, satisfies the line rule, and has every column nonconstant, contradicting <a href="#word:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:obstruction">5</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:model"></a>
### A common solution of the tiling equations

We now construct one graph satisfying all the equations of [sec:encoding,sec:activation] [sec:encoding](#sec:encoding), [sec:activation](#sec:activation). The useful output of each ordinary channel will activate exactly the symbol $f(L_i(x))$ from <a href="#word:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:model">6</a>. The high coordinates will record positions within labelled blocks. A further labelling of the horizontal residue set $S$ will let the two seed tests use a single common output $z$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Labelled blocks and their inverse map

For every channel $i$ and digit $j\in J_i$, fix a partition of $\mathbb Z/r_{ij}\mathbb Z$ into subsets of sizes $a_i$ and $b_i$, using the nonnegative representation of $r_{ij}$ chosen in <a href="#sec:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:encoding">3</a>. Label each subset of size $d\in\{a_i,b_i\}$ bijectively by $\mathbb Z/d\mathbb Z$. Copy this same partition and these same labels for every setting of the other digits in $K_i=\prod_{j'\in J_i}\mathbb Z/r_{ij'}\mathbb Z$. We obtain a partition $\mathcal B_{i,j}$ of $K_i$ into labelled blocks. A block fixes all digits except $j$; its label records a position in its $j$-digit subset. The blocks are subsets, not subgroups, and their labels need not respect addition in $K_i$.

Define a map $C_{i,j}:K_i\to P_i$ and, for each integer $t$, a permutation $T_{i,j}(t)$ of $K_i$ as follows. If $k_i$ has label $y$ in a block of size $d$, put $$C_{i,j}(k_i)=
 \begin{cases}
 (y,0),&d=a_i,\\
 (0,y),&d=b_i.
 \end{cases}$$ The permutation $T_{i,j}(t)$ sends this point to the point with label $y+t$ in the same block, with addition in $\mathbb Z/d\mathbb Z$. The map $C_{i,j}$ is independent of every digit except $j$, since the partitions and labels were copied unchanged over the other digits. It is not independent of $j$: each block has at least two positions with different useful outputs.

The following inverse calculation is the reason for the fibre orderings in <a href="#act:fibre-orderings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:fibre-orderings">[act:fibre-orderings]</a>.

<a id="stmt-model-block-inverse"></a>
<div id="model:block-inverse" class="lemma">

<a id="model:block-map"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Block inverse). *Fix $i,j$, a target horizontal coordinate $x_1\in\mathbb Z$, and a target low coordinate $k_i\in K_i$. For each $(l,\delta)\in K_i\times\Delta_i$, choose an integer $h_1(l,\delta)$ satisfying $$h_1(l,\delta)\equiv l\pmod{r_i},\qquad
 h_1(l,\delta)\equiv\rho^a(\delta)\pmod{a_i},\qquad
 h_1(l,\delta)\equiv\rho^b(\delta)\pmod{b_i}.$$ Then the map $$\label{model:block-map}
 (l,\delta)\longmapsto
 \left(
 C_{i,j}(k_i-l)+e_\delta,
 T_{i,j}\bigl(x_1-h_1(l,\delta)\bigr)(k_i-l)
 \right)$$ is a bijection from $K_i\times\Delta_i$ to $P_i\times K_i$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-block-inverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-block-inverse</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a target $((\alpha^*,\xi^*),\beta_i^*)$. The point $\beta_i^*$ specifies one block $B\in\mathcal B_{i,j}$ and its label $q$. As each $T_{i,j}(t)$ preserves its block, any preimage must have its source $k_i'=k_i-l$ in $B$.

<a id="model:a-inverse"></a>
Suppose first that $B$ has size $a_i$. Choose $\delta$ by the two conditions $$\label{model:a-inverse}
 \begin{aligned}
 e_\delta^b&=\xi^* &&\text{in }\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z,\\
 e_\delta^a+\rho^a(\delta)&=\alpha^*+x_1-q
       &&\text{in }\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z.
 \end{aligned}$$ The first fibre ordering makes this choice unique. Set $y=\alpha^*-e_\delta^a$, let $k_i'$ be the unique point of $B$ with label $y$, and set $l=k_i-k_i'$. The useful output in <a href="#model:block-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-map">[model:block-map]</a> is then $(\alpha^*,\xi^*)$. The high output has label $$y+x_1-h_1(l,\delta)
 =\alpha^*+x_1-
   \bigl(e_\delta^a+\rho^a(\delta)\bigr)
 =q
 \quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z,$$ so it is $\beta_i^*$. Conversely, both target coordinates force exactly these choices of $\delta,y,k_i'$ and $l$.

<a id="model:b-inverse"></a>
If $B$ has size $b_i$, use the second fibre ordering to choose the unique $\delta$ satisfying $$\label{model:b-inverse}
 \begin{aligned}
 e_\delta^a&=\alpha^* &&\text{in }\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z,\\
 e_\delta^b+\rho^b(\delta)&=\xi^*+x_1-q
       &&\text{in }\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z.
 \end{aligned}$$ Now $y=\xi^*-e_\delta^b$ specifies $k_i'$ in $B$, and again $l=k_i-k_i'$. The high label is $\xi^*+x_1-(e_\delta^b+\rho^b(\delta))=q$ in $\mathbb Z/b_i\mathbb Z$. These choices are necessary and sufficient, proving the bijection in both cases. ◻

</details>

<a id="model:ordinary-outputs"></a>
For an ordinary channel $i$, put $j_i(x)=f(L_i(x))$ and define $$\label{model:ordinary-outputs}
 c_i(x,k)=C_{i,j_i(x)}(k_i),\qquad
 \beta_i(x,k)=T_{i,j_i(x)}(x_1)k_i.$$ Thus its active set is exactly $\{j_i(x)\}$, and $c_i$ has the required dependence on $(L_i(x),k_i)$.

Fix a target base point $(x,k)$ in the activation test for this channel. Every batch has $h\in\mathbb Zu_i$, so its source $x'=x-h$ satisfies $L_i(x')=L_i(x)$. Consequently all these sources use the same block partition. Their low coordinates are $k_i'=k_i-l$ and their high outputs are $T_{i,j_i(x)}(x_1-h_1)k_i'$. The shear <a href="#enc:shear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:shear">[enc:shear]</a> preserves this high output when the batch adds $h_1$ to $v_i$. Hence the batch values $(c_i,\beta_i)$ are precisely <a href="#model:block-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-map">[model:block-map]</a>. By [model:block-inverse,act:fibre-criterion], the ordinary activation equation is satisfied. Its freely varied $z$-coordinate places no condition on the common output to be defined next.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Seed regions and one common output

<a id="model:seed-regions"></a>
Choose disjoint subsets $S_{\eta_1},S_{\eta_2}\subset S$ such that $$\label{model:seed-regions}
 \begin{gathered}
 \abs{S_{\eta_1}}=3,\qquad \abs{S_{\eta_2}}=4,\\
 S_{\eta_t}\subset
 \{(s_1,s_2)\in S:s_1\equiv0,\ s_2\equiv t\pmod p\}
 \qquad(t=1,2).
 \end{gathered}$$ Each of the two specified residue classes has $p^2$ points, so these choices are possible. Write $S_0=S\setminus(S_{\eta_1}\cup S_{\eta_2})$. Since $p>200$ is prime, $D=p^4\equiv1\pmod6$, and therefore $\abs{S_0}=D-7$ is divisible by $6$. Recall that $a_{\eta_1}=2$ and $a_{\eta_2}=3$.

Partition each of these three regions into label spaces according to the following table. A label space is simply a subset equipped with the displayed bijection; these are not assertions about subgroups of $S$.

<div class="center">

| Region       | Inactive seeds  | Label space                                      | Number of copies |
|:-------------|:----------------|:-------------------------------------------------|:-----------------|
| $S_{\eta_1}$ | $\eta_2$        | $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$                           | $1$              |
| $S_{\eta_2}$ | $\eta_1$        | $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$                           | $2$              |
| $S_0$        | $\eta_1,\eta_2$ | $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\times\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ | $(D-7)/6$        |

</div>

For a seed $i$ inactive at $s$, denote its label coordinate by $y_i(s)\in\mathbb Z/a_i\mathbb Z$. Thus a point of $S$ is specified by its region, the particular copy of the label space in that region, and its inactive-seed labels.

<a id="model:shared-output"></a>
For $t\in\mathbb Z$, define a permutation $R(t)$ of $S$ by preserving each label space and adding $t$ to each of its label coordinates. In particular, $R(t)$ preserves all three regions, and its inverse is $R(-t)$. Fix an arbitrary bijection $\lambda:S\to\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z$ and set $$\label{model:shared-output}
 z(x,k)=\lambda\bigl(R(x_1)s(x)\bigr).$$ Neither $\lambda$ nor $R(t)$ is required to be a homomorphism. The set $S$ only indexes the values of this one cyclic output coordinate.

<a id="model:seed-outputs"></a>
For a seed $i$, its digit set is the singleton $\{*\}$. Abbreviate $C_{i,*}$ and $T_{i,*}$ to $C_i$ and $T_i$. Define $$\label{model:seed-outputs}
 (c_i(x,k),\beta_i(x,k))=
 \begin{cases}
 \bigl(C_i(k_i),T_i(x_1)k_i\bigr),&s(x)\in S_i,\\
 \bigl((y_i(s(x)),0),k_i\bigr),&s(x)\notin S_i.
 \end{cases}$$ The useful output depends only on $(s(x),k_i)$, as required by <a href="#enc:dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:dependence">[enc:dependence]</a>. Its sole digit is active exactly when $s(x)\in S_i$.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The inverse for each seed test

<a id="model:other-seed-congruence"></a>
We verify both seed equations using the same functions <a href="#model:shared-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:shared-output">[model:shared-output]</a> and <a href="#model:seed-outputs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:seed-outputs">[model:seed-outputs]</a>. Fix a seed $i$, write $i'$ for the other seed, and fix a target base $(x,k)$. The batch indexed by $(l,\delta,\tau)$ has a predetermined offset $h=h(l,\delta,\tau)$. Its source satisfies $$x'=x-h,\qquad s'=s(x')=s(x)-\tau,\qquad k_i'=k_i-l.$$ In addition to the congruences used by <a href="#model:block-inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:block-inverse">13</a>, the seed offsets satisfy $$\label{model:other-seed-congruence}
 h_1\equiv0\pmod{a_{i'}}.$$ We will recover a unique batch from every target $$\bigl(c_i^*,\beta_i^*,z^*\bigr)
 \in P_i\times K_i\times\mathbb Z/D\mathbb Z,
 \qquad c_i^*=(\alpha^*,\xi^*).$$ By <a href="#act:fibre-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:fibre-criterion">9</a>, this is exactly the required tiling property. Put $s''=\lambda^{-1}(z^*)$. Because $R(x_1')$ preserves each label space, the points $s'$ and $s''$ must be in the same region and the same copy of its label space.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### The seed is active at the source.

Suppose $s''\in S_i$. In this region the only label coordinate belongs to the other seed $i'$. Equation <a href="#model:other-seed-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:other-seed-congruence">[model:other-seed-congruence]</a> gives $$y_{i'}(s'')=y_{i'}(s')+x_1-h_1
            =y_{i'}(s')+x_1
 \quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/a_{i'}\mathbb Z.$$ Thus $s'$ is uniquely determined by $s''$: preserve its label-space identity and subtract $x_1$ from its label. Equivalently, $s'=R(-x_1)s''$ within this region. We now know $\tau=s(x)-s'$. For this fixed $\tau$, apply <a href="#model:block-inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:block-inverse">13</a> to the offsets $h_1(l,\delta,\tau)$ and the fixed seed partition. The appropriate formula <a href="#model:a-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:a-inverse">[model:a-inverse]</a> or <a href="#model:b-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:b-inverse">[model:b-inverse]</a> recovers $\delta$; the source label then fixes $k_i'$ and $l$. The resulting source has the prescribed $s'$ and sends its $z$-coordinate to $z^*$. Every target in this case therefore has exactly one preimage.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### The seed is inactive at the source.

Suppose $s''\notin S_i$, and let $q=y_i(s'')$. The source high coordinate equals its low coordinate, so necessarily $$k_i'=\beta_i^*,\qquad l=k_i-\beta_i^*.$$ The target useful output and the rotated $i$-label impose $$e_\delta^b=\xi^*,\qquad
 y_i(s')=\alpha^*-e_\delta^a,\qquad
 q=y_i(s')+x_1-\rho^a(\delta).$$ Consequently $\delta$ is the unique index satisfying <a href="#model:a-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:a-inverse">[model:a-inverse]</a>. These equations then fix the source label $y_i(s')$. If the other seed is also inactive in this region, its source label is uniquely fixed by $$y_{i'}(s')=y_{i'}(s'')-x_1
 \quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/a_{i'}\mathbb Z,$$ using <a href="#model:other-seed-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:other-seed-congruence">[model:other-seed-congruence]</a>. If the other seed is active, there is no second label to recover. Keeping the already known label-space identity, these labels determine exactly one $s'$, and then $\tau=s(x)-s'$ fixes the remaining batch index. The offset for this recovered batch satisfies all the congruences used in the calculation, so it produces exactly the desired target. The recovery uses only the prescribed residues of $h_1$, so the offset indexed by the recovered $(l,\delta,\tau)$ has exactly the label action used to determine those indices. There is no dependence on a later choice of an integer lift. The necessity of every recovery step proves uniqueness.

For emphasis, on $S_0$ both labels are recovered on the same label space. In the test of $i$ their forward values are $$\begin{aligned}
 y_i(s'')&=\alpha^*+x_1-
                  \bigl(e_\delta^a+\rho^a(\delta)\bigr),\\
 y_{i'}(s'')&=y_{i'}(s')+x_1.
 \end{aligned}$$ The first line selects $\delta$ by the fibre ordering and then fixes $y_i(s')$; the second fixes $y_{i'}(s')$ directly. Interchanging $i$ and $i'$ proves the other seed equation with exactly the same shared output $z$. Each equation has its own unique representing batch for a target; there is no requirement that those two batches coincide.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Compatibility of all the equations

The definitions <a href="#model:ordinary-outputs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:ordinary-outputs">[model:ordinary-outputs]</a>, <a href="#model:shared-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:shared-output">[model:shared-output]</a>, and <a href="#model:seed-outputs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:seed-outputs">[model:seed-outputs]</a> specify $c_i(x,k)$ and $\beta_i(x,k)$ for every channel, as well as $z(x,k)$, at every base point $(x,k)$. By <a href="#enc:shear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:shear">[enc:shear]</a>, each chosen $\beta_i$ determines exactly one $v_i\in\mathbb Z/r_i^2\mathbb Z$ with low coordinate $k_i$. Taking these points for all $(x,k)$ defines a single set $A\subset G$ satisfying the graph equation $A\oplus H_0=G$.

The inverse calculations hold for every integer $x_1$, including negative values, since all rotations use addition in the specified finite label groups. They use only the stated residues of $h_1$, together with $s(h)=\tau$ in a seed test and $L_i(h)=0$ in an ordinary test. Thus any choices of the permitted Chinese remainder lifts, including those made to separate distinct batches, give the same verification. The actual integer $x_1$ in the high outputs and in $z$ causes no extra constraint: the dependence equations <a href="#enc:dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="enc:dependence">[enc:dependence]</a> restrict only the useful outputs $c_i$, not $\beta_i$ or $z$. The useful outputs have exactly those prescribed dependencies, and all activation equations have just been checked on this one graph.

It remains to check the exclusion equations. At every $x$, the unique active ordinary word is $$\bigl(f(L_0(x)),\ldots,f(L_{N-1}(x))\bigr),$$ which is allowed by <a href="#word:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="word:model">6</a>. Hence no forbidden ordinary word has all its labels active. If the seed $\eta_t$ is active at $x$, then <a href="#model:seed-regions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:seed-regions">[model:seed-regions]</a> gives $L_n(x)\equiv t\pmod p$ for every ordinary $n$. Since $t\in\{1,2\}$ is nonzero modulo $p$, we have $f(L_n(x))=t$. No forbidden seed and ordinary-symbol pair is simultaneously active either. [enc:exclusion](#enc:exclusion) now verifies every exclusion tile.

Combining this common solution with the obstruction already proved in <a href="#act:nonperiodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:nonperiodic">12</a> yields the required intermediate result.

<a id="stmt-model-system"></a>
<div id="model:system" class="theorem">

**Theorem 14**. *There are a finite cyclic group $V$ and finitely many nonempty finite sets $F_1,\ldots,F_s\subset G=\mathbb Z^2\times V$ for which the simultaneous tiling equations $$A\oplus F_\nu=G\qquad(1\leq\nu\leq s)$$ have a common solution, but no common solution is invariant under a finite-index subgroup of $G$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-system"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-system</summary>

*Proof.* Use the graph, dependence, exclusion, and activation tiles constructed in [sec:encoding,sec:activation] [sec:encoding](#sec:encoding), [sec:activation](#sec:activation). They are finite nonempty sets, and their finite coordinate group $V$ is cyclic. The graph constructed in this section solves all their equations. [act:nonperiodic](#act:nonperiodic) excludes every fully periodic common solution. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:stacking"></a>
### Stacking the system into one tile

We now replace the finite system of <a href="#model:system" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:system">14</a> by one translational tiling equation. The extra finite coordinate must preserve cyclicity, so we use a fresh prime cyclic group. The argument is a version of the stacking construction in Greenfeld and Tao (2024, Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GTPeriodic](#ref-GTPeriodic)], with earlier consolidation results in Greenfeld and Tao (2023, Theorem 1.15 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GTTwoTiles](#ref-GTTwoTiles)]. We give the partition and uniqueness arguments in full, choosing a fresh prime to retain the cyclic finite factor.

<a id="stmt-stack-partition"></a>
<div id="stack:partition" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (A partition with full difference sets). *For every positive integer $s$ and every finite set of primes, there is a prime $q$ outside that set and a partition $$\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z=E_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup E_s$$ into nonempty sets satisfying $E_\nu-E_\nu=\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ for every $\nu$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stack-partition"><summary>Proof of stmt-stack-partition</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a sufficiently large odd prime $q$, and color the elements of $\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ independently and uniformly with $s$ colors. Fix a nonzero $d\in\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ and a color $\nu$. Since $q$ is prime, the elements $0,d,2d,\ldots,(q-1)d$ form one cycle. The pairs $$(0,d),\ (2d,3d),\ldots,
 ((q-3)d,(q-2)d)$$ are disjoint, and the second element minus the first is $d$ in every pair. The probability that a given pair has color $\nu$ at both ends is $s^{-2}$; these events are independent across the pairs. Therefore the probability that $d\notin E_\nu-E_\nu$ is at most $$(1-s^{-2})^{\lfloor q/2\rfloor}.$$ By the union bound, the probability that some color fails to realize some nonzero difference is at most $$s(q-1)(1-s^{-2})^{\lfloor q/2\rfloor}.$$ For $s=1$ this is zero, and for fixed $s>1$ it tends to zero as $q$ tends to infinity. Choose $q$ large enough that the bound is less than one, also avoiding the specified finite set of primes. A successful coloring gives every nonzero difference in every color. Each color class is consequently nonempty, which also gives the zero difference. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stack-equivalence"></a>
<div id="stack:equivalence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Stacking and periodicity). *Let $H$ be an abelian group, let $F_1,\ldots,F_s\subset H$ be finite nonempty sets, and let $E_1,\ldots,E_s$ be a partition as in <a href="#stack:partition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:partition">15</a>. Define $$F^{\mathrm{st}}=\bigcup_{\nu=1}^s(F_\nu\times E_\nu)
 \subset H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z.$$ There is a common solution to $A\oplus F_\nu=H$ for all $\nu$ if and only if $F^{\mathrm{st}}$ tiles $H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$. Moreover, a fully periodic tiling by $F^{\mathrm{st}}$ gives a fully periodic common solution, and a fully periodic common solution gives a fully periodic tiling by $F^{\mathrm{st}}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stack-equivalence"><summary>Proof of stmt-stack-equivalence</summary>

*Proof.* If $A$ is a common solution, then $A\times\{0\}$ is a tiling set for $F^{\mathrm{st}}$: for a target $(g,t)$, the partition of $\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ selects a unique $\nu$ with $t\in E_\nu$, and the equation $A\oplus F_\nu=H$ selects a unique $g=a+f$. If $A$ has a finite-index period subgroup $P\leq H$, then $P\times\{0\}$ has finite index in $H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ and preserves this tiling set.

Conversely, suppose that $B\oplus F^{\mathrm{st}}=H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$, and fix $g\in H$. A contribution to the fibre $\{g\}\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ of color $\nu$ consists of a pair $((a,t),f)$ with $(a,t)\in B$, $f\in F_\nu$, and $a+f=g$. This contribution covers the vertical set $t+E_\nu$. There are only finitely many contributions: $a$ is determined by $f$, and there are at most $q$ possible values of $t$. Two distinct contributions of the same color cannot occur. Indeed, the identity $E_\nu-E_\nu=\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ makes any two translates of $E_\nu$ intersect, contradicting uniqueness in the stacked tiling.

<a id="stack:fibre-count"></a>
Let $n_\nu(g)\in\{0,1\}$ be the number of contributions of color $\nu$. Exact coverage of the fibre gives $$\sum_{\nu=1}^s n_\nu(g)|E_\nu|
   =q=\sum_{\nu=1}^s|E_\nu|.
 \label{stack:fibre-count}$$ Every $|E_\nu|$ is positive, so <a href="#stack:fibre-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:fibre-count">[stack:fibre-count]</a> forces $n_\nu(g)=1$ for all $\nu$.

Projection $\operatorname{pr}:H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z\to H$ is injective on $B$. Otherwise, two distinct points $(a,t),(a,t')\in B$, together with any $f\in F_1$, would give two color-$1$ contributions at $g=a+f$. Consequently $A=\operatorname{pr}(B)$ is a set of projected translations without multiplicity. The equality $n_\nu(g)=1$ now says exactly that $g$ has one representation in $A+F_\nu$. Thus $A\oplus F_\nu=H$ for every $\nu$.

Finally, if $B+P=B$ for a finite-index subgroup $P\leq H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$, then $A+\operatorname{pr}(P)=A$. The induced map $$(H\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z)/P\longrightarrow H/\operatorname{pr}(P)$$ is surjective, so $\operatorname{pr}(P)$ has finite index. This proves the periodicity assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stack-cyclic"></a>
<div id="stack:cyclic" class="corollary">

**Corollary 17** (One tile with a cyclic finite coordinate). *For some positive integer $Q$, there is a finite nonempty set $F\subset\Gamma=\mathbb Z^2\times\mathbb Z/Q\mathbb Z$ which tiles $\Gamma$ but admits no fully periodic tiling.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stack-cyclic"><summary>Proof of stmt-stack-cyclic</summary>

*Proof.* Apply <a href="#stack:equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:equivalence">16</a> to the finite system from <a href="#model:system" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:system">14</a> in $G=\mathbb Z^2\times V$. Choose the prime $q$ in <a href="#stack:partition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:partition">15</a> not to divide $|V|$. Since $V$ is cyclic, $V\times\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ is cyclic of order $Q=q|V|$: a pair of generators has order $Q$ by coprimality. The common solution gives a tiling by the stacked tile, and a fully periodic stacked tiling would give a fully periodic common solution, contrary to <a href="#model:system" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:system">14</a>. Identify the finite product with $\mathbb Z/Q\mathbb Z$ and call the stacked tile $F$. Since every system tile has cardinality $M=|H_0|$, the disjoint stacking gives $|F|=qM$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### Passing to three-dimensional space

It remains to remove the finite coordinate in <a href="#stack:cyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:cyclic">17</a>. We construct a finite tile in $\mathbb Z^3$ whose unit-cube thickening also excludes periodic tilings with arbitrary real translation vectors. Quotient-to-lattice reductions are studied by Meyerovitch, Sanadhya, and Solomon (2025, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[MSSReduction](#ref-MSSReduction)], building on earlier rigid tile constructions such as Greenfeld and Tao (2023, Lemma 9.3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GTTwoTiles](#ref-GTTwoTiles)]. The need to constrain real translation vectors also appears in the Euclidean reduction of Greenfeld and Tao (2024, Theorem 2.1 and Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GTPeriodic](#ref-GTPeriodic)]. The argument below proves both required statements directly for the same finite tile and its unit-cube thickening.

<a id="geom:representatives-U"></a>
Let $$\pi:\mathbb Z^3\longrightarrow\Gamma,\qquad
 \pi(a_1,a_2,a_3)=(a_1,a_2,a_3\bmod Q),
 \qquad w=(0,0,Q).$$ Thus $\ker\pi=\mathbb Zw$. Translate the tile $F$ of <a href="#stack:cyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:cyclic">17</a> so that $0\in F$, and choose a finite set $U\subset\mathbb Z^3$ containing $0$ on which $\pi$ is a bijection onto $F$. In particular, $$U\cap\ker\pi=\{0\},\qquad w\notin U.
 \label{geom:representatives-U}$$ Translating $F$ changes neither tileability nor the existence of a fully periodic tiling.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A residue transversal with many differences

<a id="stmt-geom-random-representatives"></a>
<div id="geom:random-representatives" class="lemma">

<a id="geom:difference-property"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Rigid representatives). *For a sufficiently large integer $m\geq2$, one can choose $$T_0=\{t(v):v\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}^3\},\qquad
 t(v)\equiv v\pmod m,$$ such that, with $T_*=T_0\setminus\{t(0)\}$, $$T_*-T_*\supset
 \{d\in\mathbb Z^3:\norm{d}_\infty\leq m,
                         \ d\notin m\mathbb Z^3\}.
 \label{geom:difference-property}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-random-representatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-random-representatives</summary>

*Proof.* Choose independently, for each residue $v$, $$t(v)=v+mh_v,\qquad
 h_v\text{ uniform in }\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}^3.$$ Fix a requested vector $d$ on the right-hand side of <a href="#geom:difference-property" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:difference-property">[geom:difference-property]</a>. Translation by $d\bmod m$ on $(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^3$ is a permutation without fixed points. A cycle of length $L\geq2$ contains $\lfloor L/2\rfloor\geq L/3$ disjoint successive pairs. Selecting these pairs in every cycle gives at least $m^3/3$ disjoint pairs of residues $(u,v)$ with $v-u\equiv d\pmod m$. Discard the pair containing residue $0$, if there is one. At least $$k_m=\lfloor m^3/3\rfloor-1$$ pairs remain, all with both endpoints nonzero.

For such a pair, the equality $t(v)-t(u)=d$ asks for $$h_v-h_u=\frac{d-(v-u)}m.$$ This is an integer vector whose coordinates have absolute value at most $2$, since $\norm{d}_\infty\leq m$ and the coordinates of $u,v$ lie between $0$ and $m-1$. Two independent uniform variables on $\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}$ have any specified difference of absolute value at most $2$ with probability at least $3/25$. Hence each chosen pair realizes $d$ with probability at least $$c=(3/25)^3=27/15625.$$ These events are independent because the pairs have disjoint endpoints. Failure to realize this $d$ has probability at most $(1-c)^{k_m}$. There are at most $(2m+1)^3$ requested vectors, so the probability that <a href="#geom:difference-property" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:difference-property">[geom:difference-property]</a> fails is at most $$(2m+1)^3(1-c)^{k_m}.$$ This tends to zero as $m$ tends to infinity. Fix $m\geq2$ for which it is less than one, and then fix a successful choice of the representatives. Every witnessing pair avoids residue $0$, as required. ◻

</details>

Define the second transversal by moving just the marked point: $$T_1=T_*\cup\{t(0)+mw\}.$$ Both $T_0$ and $T_1$ have exactly one representative of each residue modulo $m$. The switch is illustrated schematically in <a href="#geom:fig-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:fig-switch">2</a>.

<figure id="geom:fig-switch">

<figcaption>Schematic of the marked-point switch, without depicting the actual positions or scale. The gray marks represent the same common set $T_{*}$ in both rows. The marked point moves from $t(0)$ to $t(0) + mw$; the dashed outline shows its former location. The marks may also denote unit-cube thickenings. In a grid assembly this switch gives the kernel-invariance identity <a href="#geom:kernel-invariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:kernel-invariance">[geom:kernel-invariance]</a>.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-geom-fig-switch"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`geom:fig-switch`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  x=1cm,y=1cm,
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  common/.style={draw=black!55,fill=black!16,line width=.5pt},
  marked/.style={draw=BurntOrange,fill=BurntOrange!30,line width=.9pt}
]
  \node[anchor=east] at (0.8,1.35) {$T_0$};
  \node[anchor=east] at (0.8,-0.35) {$T_1$};

  \foreach \y in {1.35,-0.35} {
    \draw[common] (1.55,\y-.16) rectangle (1.87,\y+.16);
    \draw[common] (2.17,\y-.16) rectangle (2.49,\y+.16);
    \node at (2.94,\y) {$\cdots$};
    \draw[common] (3.40,\y-.16) rectangle (3.72,\y+.16);
    \node at (2.64,\y-.51) {$T_*$};
  }

  \draw[marked] (4.84,1.19) rectangle (5.16,1.51);
  \node at (5,0.84) {$t(0)$};

  \draw[draw=black!45,dashed,line width=.6pt]
    (4.84,-.51) rectangle (5.16,-.19);
  \draw[-{Latex[length=2.2mm]},line width=.8pt,BurntOrange]
    (5.40,-.35) -- node[above=3pt,text=black] {$mw$} (9.36,-.35);
  \draw[marked] (9.60,-.51) rectangle (9.92,-.19);
  \node at (9.76,-.86) {$t(0)+mw$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 22 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Schematic diagram showing two rows:
- Top row ($T_0$): labeled "$T_0$" on the left, followed by two gray solid squares, horizontal ellipsis "$\cdots$", one gray solid square (the group of gray squares is labeled "$T_*$" below), and one orange solid square labeled "$t(0)$" below.
- Bottom row ($T_1$): labeled "$T_1$" on the left, followed by the identical arrangement of gray solid squares and ellipsis labeled "$T_*$" below. At the horizontal position of $t(0)$, there is a dashed square outline. A horizontal arrow pointing right, labeled "$mw$" above, connects the dashed outline to an orange solid square labeled "$t(0) + mw$" below.
```


<a id="geom:large-tile"></a>
Our final discrete tile is $$T=T_1\cup\bigcup_{u\in U\setminus\{0\}}(mu+T_0),
 \qquad \Omega=T+[0,1]^3.
 \label{geom:large-tile}$$ The finite union defining $T$ is disjoint. To see this, equality of two points first forces equality of their residue labels $v$ modulo $m$. For $v\ne0$, the points are $mu+t(v)$, and equality forces the same $u$. For $v=0$, the unmarked points are $mu+t(0)$ with $u\in U\setminus\{0\}$, while the marked point is $mw+t(0)$. They are all distinct by <a href="#geom:representatives-U" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:representatives-U">[geom:representatives-U]</a>. In particular, $|T|=|U|m^3$.

Write $$\Omega_i=T_i+[0,1]^3\quad(i=0,1),\qquad
 \Omega_*=T_*+[0,1]^3.$$ Each $\Omega_i$ has volume $m^3$, and their common subset $\Omega_*$ has positive volume $m^3-1$. Unit cubes with distinct integer lower corners have disjoint interiors, so the decomposition of $T$ in <a href="#geom:large-tile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:large-tile">[geom:large-tile]</a> gives the corresponding decomposition of $\Omega$ up to null boundaries. We call the translation vector $c$ the center of the copy $c+T_i$ or $c+\Omega_i$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Existence of a tiling

<a id="stmt-geom-existence"></a>
<div id="geom:existence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19**. *The tile $T$ tiles $\mathbb Z^3$, and $\Omega$ tiles $\mathbb R^3$ up to null sets.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-existence"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-existence</summary>

*Proof.* Let $A\oplus F=\Gamma$ be a tiling from <a href="#stack:cyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:cyclic">17</a>, and put $B=\pi^{-1}(A)$. Then $B+w=B$. Also $B\oplus U=\mathbb Z^3$: for any $g\in\mathbb Z^3$, the unique representation of $\pi(g)$ in $A+F$ determines a unique $u\in U$, and then $g-u\in B$ is the unique possible other summand.

Place copies of $T$ at all translations in $mB$. Their constituent copies of $T_0$ or $T_1$ have centers $m(b+u)$, for $b\in B$ and $u\in U$. Since $B\oplus U=\mathbb Z^3$, there is exactly one such center at every point of $m\mathbb Z^3$. Its type is $1$ precisely at the centers $mB$, because the type-$1$ piece corresponds to $u=0$.

The assembly with $T_0$ at every center in $m\mathbb Z^3$ tiles $\mathbb Z^3$ exactly: each integer vector has a unique residue label $v$ and a unique expression $ma+t(v)$. Switching to type $1$ at the centers $mB$ removes the marked points $$t(0)+mB$$ and inserts $$t(0)+m(B+w)=t(0)+mB.$$ The insertion is a bijective relocation, so all multiplicities remain one. This proves $mB\oplus T=\mathbb Z^3$. Thickening by unit cubes gives an almost-everywhere tiling by $\Omega$ with the same translation set: away from the integer coordinate planes, every point belongs to the interior of one integer unit cube. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Rigidity for real translation vectors

We next show why arbitrary real translations cannot produce a periodic tiling. The marked-point modification has two roles. The common part forces the centers of the small pieces onto one grid, and the moved point then forces invariance under the kernel direction $w$.

<a id="stmt-geom-rounding"></a>
<div id="geom:rounding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Rounding in the closed box). *Let $m\geq2$, and suppose that $\delta\in\mathbb R^3$ satisfies $\norm{\delta}_\infty\leq m$ and $\delta\notin m\mathbb Z^3$. There is $d\in\mathbb Z^3\setminus m\mathbb Z^3$ such that $$\norm{d}_\infty\leq m,
 \qquad \norm{\delta-d}_\infty<1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-rounding"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-rounding</summary>

*Proof.* If $\delta$ is integral, use $d=\delta$. Otherwise, keep every integer coordinate unchanged and round each noninteger coordinate either down or up. Both choices lie in $[-m,m]$, since the endpoints are integers, and both are at distance strictly less than one from the original coordinate. In at least one noninteger coordinate choose a rounding which is not a multiple of $m$. Such a choice exists because two consecutive integers cannot both be multiples of $m\geq2$. The resulting $d$ is not in $m\mathbb Z^3$. Coordinates of $\delta$ equal to $m$ or $-m$ are left unchanged, so the strict distance estimate also holds on the boundary of the box. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-geom-grid"></a>
<div id="geom:grid" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (A periodic assembly has grid centers). *Suppose copies $c+\Omega_{\iota(c)}$, with types $\iota(c)\in\{0,1\}$, form an almost-everywhere tiling of $\mathbb R^3$. Assume the assembly, including its types, is invariant under a full-rank lattice $\Lambda\subset\mathbb R^3$. Then the set $C$ of centers is $c_0+m\mathbb Z^3$ for some $c_0\in\mathbb R^3$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-grid"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-grid</summary>

<a id="geom:nearby-centers"></a>
*Proof.* First, two constituent copies cannot have the same center, because both contain the positive-volume common part $\Omega_*$. Thus the centers and their types are unambiguous. We claim that distinct centers $c,c'$ satisfy $$\norm{c'-c}_\infty\leq m
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad c'-c\in m\mathbb Z^3.
 \label{geom:nearby-centers}$$ If $\delta=c'-c$ violated this assertion, <a href="#geom:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:rounding">20</a> would give a vector $d$ in the difference set in <a href="#geom:difference-property" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:difference-property">[geom:difference-property]</a> with $\norm{\delta-d}_\infty<1$. Write $d=t-t'$ for $t,t'\in T_*$. The two common-part unit cubes $$c+t+[0,1]^3,\qquad c'+t'+[0,1]^3$$ have lower corners whose coordinate differences have absolute value strictly less than one. Their intersection therefore has positive volume, contradicting the tiling property. This proves <a href="#geom:nearby-centers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:nearby-centers">[geom:nearby-centers]</a>.

In particular, distinct centers have sup-norm distance at least $m$: a nonzero vector in $m\mathbb Z^3$ cannot have smaller sup norm. The set $C$ is consequently uniformly separated and locally finite. The auxiliary closed cubes $$Q_c=c+[-m/2,m/2]^3\qquad(c\in C)$$ have disjoint interiors. Each has volume $m^3$, equal to the volume of either constituent type.

We use periodicity to show that these auxiliary cubes cover. Let $P$ be a bounded half-open fundamental parallelepiped for $\Lambda$. There are finitely many centers modulo $\Lambda$, by local finiteness; choose representatives $c_1,\ldots,c_r$. Their types $\iota_1,\ldots,\iota_r$ are preserved under $\Lambda$. Integrating the constituent tiling over $P$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |P|
 &=\int_P\sum_{j=1}^r\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
       \mathbf{1}_{c_j+\lambda+\Omega_{\iota_j}}(x)\,dx\\
 &=\sum_{j=1}^r|\Omega_{\iota_j}|=rm^3.
\end{aligned}$$ For the second equality, translate the integration domains by $-\lambda$ and use that $\{P-\lambda:\lambda\in\Lambda\}$ partitions $\mathbb R^3$ up to boundaries. Nonnegative summands justify the exchange of summation and integration.

The same calculation for the auxiliary cubes gives $$\int_P\sum_{c\in C}\mathbf{1}_{Q_c}(x)\,dx=rm^3=|P|.$$ The integrand is at most one almost everywhere, because the cubes have disjoint interiors; their countably many boundaries are null. It is therefore one almost everywhere on $P$, and periodicity gives almost-everywhere coverage of $\mathbb R^3$. The family of closed cubes $\{Q_c\}_{c\in C}$ is locally finite, so its union is closed. A point outside the union would have an open neighborhood of positive volume outside it, contradicting almost-everywhere coverage. Thus the cubes cover every point of $\mathbb R^3$.

Their intersection graph is connected. Indeed, if one graph component were separated from the remaining components, the unions of their respective cubes would be disjoint nonempty closed sets covering $\mathbb R^3$. Both unions are closed because they are subfamilies of a locally finite family of closed sets. They would disconnect $\mathbb R^3$, which is impossible. Whenever $Q_c$ and $Q_{c'}$ intersect, $\norm{c'-c}_\infty\leq m$, so <a href="#geom:nearby-centers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:nearby-centers">[geom:nearby-centers]</a> puts their difference in $m\mathbb Z^3$. Following paths in the intersection graph therefore puts every center in one coset $c_0+m\mathbb Z^3$.

No point of that coset can be missing. The interior of the side-$m$ cube at a missing grid point is disjoint from all side-$m$ cubes at the other grid points and would be uncovered. Hence $C=c_0+m\mathbb Z^3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Kernel invariance and descent

<a id="stmt-geom-no-periodic"></a>
<div id="geom:no-periodic" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22**. *The Euclidean tile $\Omega$ has no almost-everywhere translational tiling whose translation set is invariant under a full-rank lattice in $\mathbb R^3$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-no-periodic"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-no-periodic</summary>

<a id="geom:center-decomposition"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose, to the contrary, that $\mathcal A\subset\mathbb R^3$ is such a translation set and that $\mathcal A+\Lambda=\mathcal A$ for a full-rank lattice $\Lambda$. Distinct vectors of $\mathcal A$ have sup-norm distance at least one: otherwise their translates of a fixed unit cube in $\Omega$ would overlap in positive volume. Hence $\mathcal A$ is countable. Decompose every translated copy of $\Omega$ using <a href="#geom:large-tile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:large-tile">[geom:large-tile]</a>. A copy at $a\in\mathcal A$ has a type-$1$ constituent centered at $a$ and type-$0$ constituents centered at $a+mu$ for $u\in U\setminus\{0\}$. The constituents form an almost-everywhere tiling and inherit the lattice periods, including their types. No two constituents have the same center, as observed in the proof of <a href="#geom:grid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:grid">21</a>. Consequently the map $$\mathcal A\times U\longrightarrow C,\qquad(a,u)\longmapsto a+mu
 \label{geom:center-decomposition}$$ is a bijection, and the type-$1$ centers are exactly $\mathcal A$.

<a id="geom:upstairs-tiling"></a>
By <a href="#geom:grid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:grid">21</a>, $C=c_0+m\mathbb Z^3$. Translate the entire assembly by $-c_0$, which does not change its period lattice, and henceforth write $C=m\mathbb Z^3$. There is a set $B'\subset\mathbb Z^3$ such that $\mathcal A=mB'$. Let $b(a)=\mathbf{1}_{B'}(a)$; this records the type at the center $ma$. The bijection <a href="#geom:center-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:center-decomposition">[geom:center-decomposition]</a>, divided by $m$, gives $$B'\oplus U=\mathbb Z^3.
 \label{geom:upstairs-tiling}$$

<a id="geom:kernel-invariance"></a>
Consider the open unit cell with lower corner $t(0)+ma$, for any $a\in\mathbb Z^3$. Since all constituent centers and all voxel positions are integral, a unit cube can meet the interior of this cell only if it has the same lower corner. The residues modulo $m$ then exclude every common-part point $t(v)$ with $v\ne0$. There are exactly two possible sources: the unshifted marked point from type $0$ at $ma$, or the moved marked point from type $1$ at $m(a-w)$. The coverage multiplicity on this open cell is therefore $$1-b(a)+b(a-w).$$ The almost-everywhere tiling makes it one. Thus $b(a)=b(a-w)$ for every $a$, or equivalently $$B'+w=B'.
 \label{geom:kernel-invariance}$$ This also gives invariance under every element of $\ker\pi=\mathbb Zw$.

The set $B'$ is fully periodic in the discrete sense. It is nonempty, and $\mathcal A=mB'\subset m\mathbb Z^3$. For $a_0\in\mathcal A$ and any $\lambda\in\Lambda$, both $a_0$ and $a_0+\lambda$ belong to $m\mathbb Z^3$, so $\lambda\in m\mathbb Z^3$. Hence $L=m^{-1}\Lambda$ is a rank-three lattice contained in $\mathbb Z^3$ and preserves $B'$. Such an integer lattice has finite index: the matrix formed by an integer basis has nonzero integer determinant, whose absolute value is its index. In particular, $L\leq\mathbb Z^3$ is a finite-index period subgroup of $B'$.

We finally descend the exact tiling <a href="#geom:upstairs-tiling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:upstairs-tiling">[geom:upstairs-tiling]</a> to the quotient. Fix $\gamma\in\Gamma$ and any lift $g\in\mathbb Z^3$. For each $u\in U$, there is a representation $$\gamma=\alpha+\pi(u),\qquad \alpha\in\pi(B'),$$ if and only if $g-u\in B'$. One direction follows by applying $\pi$. For the other, if $\alpha=\pi(b_0)$ with $b_0\in B'$, then $g-u-b_0\in\ker\pi$; <a href="#geom:kernel-invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:kernel-invariance">[geom:kernel-invariance]</a> therefore puts $g-u$ in $B'$. By <a href="#geom:upstairs-tiling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:upstairs-tiling">[geom:upstairs-tiling]</a>, exactly one $u$ has this property, and $\pi$ is injective on $U$. Thus $$\pi(B')\oplus\pi(U)=\Gamma.$$ This quotient tiling is fully periodic. Indeed, $\pi(L)$ preserves $\pi(B')$, and the surjection $\mathbb Z^3/L\to\Gamma/\pi(L)$ shows that $\pi(L)$ has finite index. Since $\pi(U)=F$, this contradicts <a href="#stack:cyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:cyclic">17</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-no-periodic-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-no-periodic</summary>

*Completion of the proof of <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The finite nonempty tile $T$ from <a href="#geom:large-tile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:large-tile">[geom:large-tile]</a> and its closed unit-cube thickening $\Omega$ admit the tilings in <a href="#geom:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:existence">19</a>. The Euclidean tiling cannot be fully periodic by <a href="#geom:no-periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:no-periodic">22</a>. If a discrete tiling of $T$ had a finite-index period subgroup in $\mathbb Z^3$, thickening its unit cells would give an almost-everywhere tiling of $\Omega$ invariant under the same subgroup, viewed as a full-rank lattice in $\mathbb R^3$. This is also excluded by <a href="#geom:no-periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:no-periodic">22</a>, proving both claims. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 23* (Finite selection of the tile data). The finite data in the construction can be selected by terminating finite searches. The primes can be selected successively by trial division. The tiling tests involve maps between finite sets. Lemmas <a href="#stack:partition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stack:partition">15</a> and <a href="#geom:random-representatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:random-representatives">18</a> give explicit inequalities under which a suitable finite coloring or residue transversal exists; exhaustive search then selects one. This describes the finite tile itself. The infinite tiling that proves existence is instead specified by the last-nonzero-digit function. No practical bound on the size of the finite construction is required.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### References

<div id="refs" class="references csl-bib-body hanging-indent">

Bhattacharya, Siddhartha. 2020. “Periodicity and Decidability of Tilings of $\mathbb{Z}^2$.” *American Journal of Mathematics* 142 (1): 255–66. <https://doi.org/10.1353/ajm.2020.0006>.

</div>

<div id="ref-RationalPolygons" class="csl-entry">

      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  every node/.style={font=\\small,align=center},\n  stage/.style={draw=MidnightBlue!65,fill=MidnightBlue!4,\n    rounded corners=2pt,text width=3.15cm,minimum height=1.75cm,inner sep=4pt},\n  route/.style={-{Latex[length=2mm]},line width=.7pt}\n]\n\\node[stage] (word) {Word arrays\\\\with nonconstant\\\\columns\\\\\\cref{sec:word}};\n\\node[stage,right=5mm of word] (system) {Common solution\\\\in $\\Z^2\\times V$\\\\$V$ cyclic\\\\\\cref{sec:encoding,sec:activation,sec:model}};\n\\node[stage,right=5mm of system] (stack) {One tile $F$\\\\in $\\Z^2\\times\\Zmod Q$\\\\\\cref{sec:stacking}};\n\\node[stage,right=5mm of stack] (space) {One finite tile $T$\\\\and its thickening\\\\$T+[0,1]^3$\\\\\\cref{sec:geometry}};\n\\draw[route] (word) -- (system);\n\\draw[route] (system) -- (stack);\n\\draw[route] (stack) -- (space);\n\\draw[route,MidnightBlue] ([yshift=4mm]word.north west) --\n  node[above=1pt] {construct a solution, then a tiling}\n  ([yshift=4mm]space.north east);\n\\draw[route,BurntOrange] ([yshift=-4mm]space.south east) --\n  node[below=1pt] {a fully periodic tiling would give a forbidden vertical period}\n  ([yshift=-4mm]word.south west);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A flow diagram showing four cyan-outlined rectangular boxes arranged horizontally and connected left-to-right with arrows.\nAbove the boxes, a long teal horizontal arrow pointing to the right is labeled \"construct a solution, then a tiling\".\nBelow the boxes, a long orange/gold horizontal arrow pointing to the left is labeled \"a fully periodic tiling would give a forbidden vertical period\".\nThe contents of the four boxes from left to right are:\n- Box 1: \"Word arrays with nonconstant columns Section 2\"\n- Box 2: \"Common solution in $\\mathbb{Z}^2 \\times V$ $V$ cyclic Sections 3 to 5\"\n- Box 3: \"One tile $F$ in $\\mathbb{Z}^2 \\times \\mathbb{Z}/Q\\mathbb{Z}$ Section 6\"\n- Box 4: \"One finite tile $T$ and its thickening $T + [0, 1]^3$ Section 7\"\nArrows connect Box 1 to Box 2, Box 2 to Box 3, and Box 3 to Box 4."
    },
